```text
49	\paragraph{Nearest-neighbor matching.} The 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching selects, for each focal-cohort model, the reference-cohort model with the closest total score (no caliper). Balance is verified by Cohen's $d = -0.17$ on matched pairs ($N = 638$ per group), confirming minimal residual ability confounding.
50	
51	\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (\Cref{sec:temporal}) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (\Cref{sec:structural}) uses logistic regression with a group $\times$ ability interaction term; a significant interaction ($p < 0.05$) indicates non-uniform DIF.
```

Post-matching balance is verified via Cohen's $d$ ($= -0.17$ on total score; $N = 638$ per group), confirming minimal residual ability confounding. The reduced sample size (approximately 50\\% of the original) is accounted for in the interpretation of statistical power.","originalFile":"\\section{Method Details}\n\\label{app:method_details}\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostics}\n\\label{app:diagnostics}\n\nThree diagnostic procedures complement the primary DIF analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Local independence (Yen's Q3).} The MH procedure assumes that item responses are conditionally independent given $\\theta$. Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most. At the ecological level, we compute Spearman $\\rho$ between subject-level C\\% and subject-level accuracy improvement from 2023 to 2024. At the item level, logistic regression predicts item-level DIF~C classification from domain-level accuracy change, with and without item property controls (difficulty, discrimination). This decomposition quantifies how much of the observed instability is attributable to aggregate domain-level gains versus item-specific behavioral change.\n\n\\paragraph{Instability decomposition.} When domain accuracy improvement is included alongside item-level controls, we compute the counterfactual C\\% that would remain after removing domain-level effects. The gap between observed and counterfactual C\\% measures the contribution of domain-level improvement to the total instability.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Considerations}\n\\label{app:statistical}\n\n\\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\in \\{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT model fit.} For transparency, we report that the MMLU response matrix shows poor fit to a unidimensional 2PL model (expected for a 57-subject multidimensional benchmark). This does not affect MH-DIF validity, which relies only on total score stratification rather than parametric IRT assumptions.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.\n\n\\paragraph{Power sensitivity to item discrimination.}\n\\label{app:power_sensitivity}\nThe Monte Carlo power analysis in \\Cref{sec:validation} uses average 2PL parameters ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$). \\Cref{tab:power_sensitivity} reports TPR as a function of discrimination $a$ (other parameters at their means, $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications). TPR increases monotonically from 0.40 ($a = 0.5$) to 0.95 ($a = 2.0$); FPR remains below 0.04 throughout. The MMLU item pool shows substantial heterogeneity: $a$ ranges from 0.006 to 2.657 (SD $= 0.54$, IQR $= [0.75, 1.64]$). The aggregate TPR of 0.68 from Phase~2 reflects this full distribution, while TPR at mean $a \\approx 1.2$ is 0.87---the gap is consistent with Jensen's inequality applied to the concave TPR($a$) function. Although 2PL fit is poor for MMLU's multidimensional structure, the power analysis serves as one leg of a triangulation: parametric estimates (TPR $= 0.68$--$0.80$) are validated by empirical semi-synthetic injection ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}), confirming adequate detection power regardless of parametric assumptions.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Power sensitivity to item discrimination. TPR = true positive rate for ETS~C detection ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$) at $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications.}\n\\label{tab:power_sensitivity}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rccc}\n\\toprule\n$a$ & TPR & 95\\% CI & FPR \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.5 & 0.398 & [0.20, 0.60] & 0.033 \\\\\n0.8 & 0.683 & [0.45, 0.90] & 0.036 \\\\\n1.0 & 0.808 & [0.60, 0.95] & 0.038 \\\\\n1.2 ($\\approx \\bar{a}$) & 0.874 & [0.70, 1.00] & 0.038 \\\\\n1.5 & 0.930 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.038 \\\\\n2.0 & 0.949 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.037 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Nearest-neighbor matching.} The 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching selects, for each focal-cohort model, the reference-cohort model with the closest total score (no caliper). Balance is verified by Cohen's $d = -0.17$ on matched pairs ($N = 638$ per group), confirming minimal residual ability confounding.\n\n\\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (\\Cref{sec:temporal}) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (\\Cref{sec:structural}) uses logistic regression with a group $\\times$ ability interaction term; a significant interaction ($p < 0.05$) indicates non-uniform DIF.\n\n\\section{Structural Analysis Details}\n\\label{app:structural}\n\nTwo structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce the temporal C\\% below 31\\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal C\\% unchanged at approximately 0.31. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\\% $= 38.1\\%$, exceeding the 25\\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. As a sensitivity check, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$ and C\\% $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. \\Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.\n\nThe temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\\ 2.2\\% cross-subject; $5{\\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \\times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions available in the supplementary material).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Domain-level Cohen's $d$ between 2023 and 2024 cohorts. The narrow range ($0.12$) indicates nearly uniform improvement across domains, supporting total-score matching adequacy for DIF analysis (\\Cref{sec:method}).}\n\\label{tab:domain_cohens_d}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lr}\n\\toprule\nDomain & Cohen's $d$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSTEM & 0.261 \\\\\nHumanities & 0.159 \\\\\nSocial Science & 0.147 \\\\\nOther & 0.146 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Multi-subject injection robustness. $k$ = number of simultaneously contaminated subjects (out of 57); values are mean $\\pm$ SD across 5 random subject draws at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$.}\n\\label{tab:multi_subject_injection}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcc}\n\\toprule\n$k$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & $0.000_{\\pm.000}$ & $0.371_{\\pm.048}$ \\\\\n3 & $0.003_{\\pm.002}$ & $0.385_{\\pm.031}$ \\\\\n5 & $0.007_{\\pm.003}$ & $0.392_{\\pm.027}$ \\\\\n10 & $0.047_{\\pm.022}$ & $0.401_{\\pm.024}$ \\\\\n20 & $0.069_{\\pm.018}$ & $0.410_{\\pm.020}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Leave-one-family-out (LOFO) robustness on MMLU. Removing non-dominant families ($\\leq$15\\% of their cohort) changes C\\% by at most 2.93\\,pp. Larger shifts from removing Mistral or Llama-3 are driven almost entirely by family composition, not statistical power loss: Monte Carlo random-subsample calibration (200 replicates per scenario, matching the reduced sample size) shows the power effect is $\\leq$1\\,pp, while the composition effect accounts for 95--113\\% of the observed C\\% drop (Mistral: $-8.44$\\,pp composition vs.\\ $-0.41$\\,pp power; Llama-3: $-8.17$\\,pp vs.\\ ${+}0.91$\\,pp; observed C\\% falls below the 95\\% random-subsample range in both cases). Stratification uses total test score for computational efficiency; cross-subject matching yields identical baseline C\\%.}\n\\label{tab:lofo}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrr}\n\\toprule\nFamily & $N$ removed & Cohort share & C\\% (LOFO) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\emph{Full sample} & --- & --- & 31.32 \\\\\nMistral & 547 & 51\\% ref & 22.47 \\\\\nLlama-3 & 315 & 39\\% foc & 24.06 \\\\\nMixtral & 120 & 15\\% foc & 34.26 \\\\\nLlama-2 & 161 & 15\\% ref & 31.48 \\\\\nSolar & 91 & 11\\% foc & 31.01 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Design Effect Estimation}\n\\label{app:deff}\n\nModel families (e.g., Mistral, Llama-3) induce intra-class correlation (ICC) that inflates Mantel-Haenszel $\\chi^2$ statistics beyond the nominal $\\chi^2_1$ reference distribution. We estimate the design effect (DEFF) per item following \\citet{rao1992variance}: $\\mathrm{DEFF}_i = 1 + (\\bar{m} - 1) \\cdot \\mathrm{ICC}_i$, where $\\bar{m}$ is the mean family size and $\\mathrm{ICC}_i$ is the one-way random-effects ICC for item $i$ across 30 families (restricted to ref+foc cohort; 1,884 models with family$\\geq$2).\n\nTwo estimates of $\\bar{m}$ bracket the adjustment: (1)~\\emph{Simple mean} ($\\bar{m} = 62.8$), treating all families equally, yields $\\mathrm{DEFF} = 3.87$ and adjusted C\\% $= 29.2\\%$; (2)~\\emph{Weighted mean} ($\\bar{m} = \\sum n_f^2 / \\sum n_f = 254.5$; \\citealp{kish1965survey}), reflecting the effective cluster size experienced by a randomly sampled model, yields $\\mathrm{DEFF} = 12.79$ and adjusted C\\% $= 13.5\\%$.\n\n\\noindent Mean ICC $= 0.047$ (median $0.041$, Q3 $= 0.061$, max $= 0.219$; 4.8\\% of items exceed $0.10$). After dividing each item's $\\chi^2_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ by its item-specific DEFF and re-applying ETS thresholds, 13.5--29.2\\% of items retain Category~C classification depending on the $\\bar{m}$ variant---both far exceeding the 0.18\\% random-split baseline (75--162$\\times$).\n\n\\section{Uncertainty Quantification Comparison}\n\\label{app:uncertainty}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Comparison of uncertainty quantification methods for the 31.3\\% C\\% estimate on MMLU.}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llr}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Key assumption & Result \\\\\n\\midrule\nModel-level bootstrap & Models independent & 95\\% CI [29.4\\%, 38.1\\%] \\\\\nFamily-level cluster bootstrap & Family is clustering unit & 95\\% CI [26.7\\%, 53.2\\%] \\\\\nDEFF (simple $\\bar{m}$) & Equal-weight families & Adjusted C\\% = 29.2\\% \\\\\nDEFF (weighted $\\bar{m}$) & Size-proportional weighting & Adjusted C\\% = 13.5\\% \\\\\nGraduated de-dup.\\ ($K{=}20$) & Remove within-family duplicates & C\\% = 19.7\\% $\\pm$ 0.9\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\label{tab:uncertainty}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe cluster bootstrap upper bound (53.2\\%) is driven by extreme bootstrap resamples that over-represent Mistral ($n = 547$) or Llama-3 ($n = 315$), producing atypical cohort compositions. Two independent methods---cluster bootstrap lower bound (26.7\\%) and simple-mean DEFF adjustment (29.2\\%)---converge on $\\sim$27--29\\%, establishing a robust corrected estimate that remains $>$150$\\times$ the random-split baseline (0.18\\%). Graduated de-duplication at $K{=}20$ yields C\\% $= 19.7\\% \\pm 0.9\\%$ (random member selection, 5 draws), lower than the corrected estimate due to reduced statistical power ($N \\approx 450$ vs.\\ 1,887), but within-$K$ variance ${<}1.5$\\,pp at all $K$ confirms the signal is power-driven rather than redundancy-inflated (\\Cref{app:dedup_k}). The weighted-mean DEFF (13.5\\%) represents a conservative extreme; even this lower bound exceeds the baseline by 75$\\times$.\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}\n\\label{app:gsm8k_details}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-benchmark comparison of DIF analysis on MMLU and GSM8K. Both benchmarks exhibit temporal measurement instability with validated semi-synthetic detection power. Standard and external matching use different exploitation rates ($p$) because each benchmark's optimal operating point differs; external matching results are compared at $p = 0.5$ for both. Subscript $\\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 random injection seeds.}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k_comparison}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nItems & 12,508 & 1,319 \\\\\nModels & 5,227 & 6,702 \\\\\nTemporal C\\% & 31.3\\% & 18.9\\% \\\\\nSanity C\\% & 0.18\\% & 0.3\\% \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.7$, standard) & -- & $+0.818_{\\pm.006}$ \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.5$, external) & $+0.508_{\\pm.027}$ & $+0.514_{\\pm.028}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{app:figures}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}\n\\label{app:fig_matching}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_4_downstream_impact.pdf}\n\\caption{Downstream impact of DIF-based item filtering on model rankings. Despite $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ overall, individual model families show systematic rank shifts when evaluated on stable items only.}\n\\label{app:fig_downstream}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\n\\caption{Exploratory: GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$; small sample, results should be interpreted cautiously). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items. Three of 33 models exceed the Cook's $D$ threshold ($4/N = 0.12$); leave-one-out analysis confirms all individual-removal correlations remain significant ($r \\in [-0.54, -0.37]$, all $p < 0.05$), and removing all three outliers yields $r = -0.38$ ($p = 0.036$, $N = 30$).}\n\\label{app:fig_gsm1k}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf}\n\\caption{Ranking degradation under DIF-ordered vs.\\ random item removal across six benchmarks. DIF-ordered removal (orange) consistently degrades $\\rho$ faster than random removal (blue band = mean $\\pm$ 1 SD, 20 seeds). Vertical dashed lines mark the Monitor/Flag (15\\%) and Flag/Act (25\\%) thresholds from \\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}.}\n\\label{app:fig_rho_degradation}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Item-Property Predictive Analysis}\n\\label{app:predictive}\n\nTo test whether DIF susceptibility is a fixed item property, we trained logistic regression and LightGBM classifiers to predict ETS~C classification from 17 leak-free features: ref-cohort difficulty and discrimination, 11 text features (question/option length, vocabulary metrics), subject metadata, and web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Features were computed exclusively from the reference (2023) cohort to prevent information leakage from the focal cohort. Five-fold subject-stratified cross-validation (no within-subject train/test overlap) yielded AUROC $= 0.603 \\pm 0.020$ (LR) and $0.599 \\pm 0.026$ (LightGBM)---near chance. Web-overlap labels contributed negligibly (SHAP $< 0.015$), independently confirming the DIF--contamination orthogonality reported in \\Cref{sec:groundtruth}. These results indicate that temporal DIF reflects emergent cohort--item interactions rather than fixed item defects, motivating post-hoc monitoring over pre-screening.\n\n\\section{Within-Family Similarity by Family Size}\n\\label{app:icc_family}\n\nWithin-family similarity (mean pairwise Pearson $r$ over 12{,}508-item response vectors) shows no dependence on family size (Spearman $\\rho = -0.009$, $p = 0.96$; 30 families with $\\geq 2$ members, 1{,}884 models). Large families (Mistral, $m = 547$, $\\bar{r} = 0.47$; Llama-3, $m = 315$, $\\bar{r} = 0.53$) are no more homogeneous than small or mid-sized families. Of the 5{,}227 models, 3{,}343 lack family assignments; these are retained in DIF analysis but excluded from similarity estimation. Within the temporal DIF cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} + N_{\\mathrm{foc}} = 1{,}887$), 1{,}884 (99.8\\%) belong to identified families with $\\geq 2$ members, so DEFF estimates are computed over nearly the full cohort. This indicates that the weighted-mean DEFF variant's effective cluster size ($\\bar{m} = 254$) reflects an arithmetic artifact of mega-family dominance rather than genuine within-family homogeneity.\n\n\\section{Graduated De-Duplication Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:dedup_k}\n\nFor each value of $K$ (max models retained per family), $K$ members are sampled uniformly at random; remaining family members are excluded from both reference and focal cohorts, and MH-DIF is recomputed on the reduced sample (5 independent draws per $K$; $K = \\text{all}$ is deterministic).\n\nC\\% increases monotonically from 0.3\\% ($K = 1$) to 31.3\\% ($K = \\text{all}$). At each fixed $K$, variance across random member draws is negligible (SD ${<} 1.5$\\,pp), confirming that the signal scales with statistical power rather than within-family redundancy. At $K{=}20$, C\\% $= 19.7\\% \\pm 0.9\\%$ ($N \\approx 450$); the gap from the full-sample 31.3\\% reflects reduced power, not reduced bias.\n\n\\section{Rank-Reversal by Distance}\n\\label{app:rank_reversal}\n\nPairwise rank reversals between full-item and stable-item rankings concentrate among closely-ranked models. At $\\Delta\\mathrm{rank} < 50$ (mean $\\Delta\\mathrm{acc} = 0.003$, effectively statistical ties), the reversal rate is 35.2\\%. It drops to 15.1\\% for $\\Delta\\mathrm{rank} \\in [50, 200)$, 4.0\\% for $[200, 500)$, 0.6\\% for $[500, 1000)$, and 0.0\\% above 1000. Broad leaderboard structure is fully preserved; instability affects only models within each other's noise margin.\n\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":46,"oldLines":7,"newStart":46,"newLines":7,"lines":[" \\end{tabular}"," \\end{table}"," ","-\\paragraph{Nearest-neighbor matching.} The 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching selects, for each focal-cohort model, the reference-cohort model with the closest total score (no caliper). Balance is verified by Cohen's $d = -0.17$ on matched pairs ($N = 638$ per group), confirming minimal residual ability confounding.","+\\paragraph{Nearest-neighbor matching.} We employ 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score with a caliper of 0.2~SD. Post-matching balance is verified via Cohen's $d$ ($= -0.17$ on total score; $N = 638$ per group), confirming minimal residual ability confounding. The reduced sample size (approximately 50\\% of the original) is accounted for in the interpretation of statistical power."," "," \\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (\\Cref{sec:temporal}) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (\\Cref{sec:structural}) uses logistic regression with a group $\\times$ ability interaction term; a significant interaction ($p < 0.05$) indicates non-uniform DIF."," "]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

```text
53	{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01HSPHiC6gM29GRiARhNr7UF","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t\\section{Discussion}\n2\t\\label{sec:discussion}\n3\t\\enlargethispage*{4\\baselineskip}\n4\t% TODO: verify page break after final edits\n5\t\n6\t\\subsection{What Drives Temporal DIF?}\n7\t\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n8\t\n9\t\\looseness=-1\n10\tTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}. Three non-exclusive mechanisms may drive the observed ${\\approx}$27--29\\% rate: (1)~differential contamination; (2)~capability evolution; (3)~training methodology shifts. The ETS baseline of 5--15\\% C\\%~\\citep{clauser1998dif} is for concurrent demographic groups; the comparison to temporal LLM cohorts is illustrative, not normative.\n11\t\n12\t\\looseness=-1\n13\tFour exclusion arguments constrain this ambiguity: ability confounds explain at most 2.4~pp (matched C\\% $= 28.9\\%$; \\Cref{sec:temporal}); domain-level improvement explains ${<}0.1\\%$ of item-level DIF variance ($R^2 = 0.0007$); no enrichment among expert-annotated errors~\\citep{gema2024mmlu} ($= 1.10$, $p = 0.23$); web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$; \\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n14\t\n15\t\\looseness=-1\n16\tThe aggregate difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$, robust at $[0.2, 0.8]$: $\\rho = -0.447$, $p < 10^{-100}$; \\Cref{sec:temporal}) is suggestive but not diagnostic: semi-synthetic analysis (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}) shows uniform injection also produces $\\rho \\approx -0.52$ via ceiling effects, while difficulty-dependent injection yields $\\rho \\approx +0.77$ (zero overlap across seeds); the observed $\\rho$ cannot alone distinguish contamination from capability evolution~\\citep{suk2026rethinking}. The aggregate $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ masks subject-level instability (22/57 below 0.95; five below 0.90), motivating targeted replacement~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf,habba2026growingpains}.\n17\t\n18\t\\looseness=-1\n19\tStratifying by model type reveals distinct mechanisms: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring with a strong difficulty gradient ($\\rho = -0.570$), consistent with differential memorization decay, while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring with a near-zero gradient ($\\rho = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$), consistent with uniform alignment improvements. The aggregate gradient is driven by base models; the instruct flat gradient is compatible with either capability gains or difficulty-independent memorization. This decomposition provides positive evidence for mechanism separation, complementing the exclusion arguments above. An alternative explanation---that simple items are uniformly contaminated ($\\to$ DIF-A) while complex items are differentially contaminated ($\\to$ DIF-C)---is partially addressed by this base-vs-instruct direction reversal: under a pure contamination account, both model types should exhibit the same DIF direction, yet we observe opposite patterns (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\n20\t\n21\t\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n22\t\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n23\t\n24\t\\looseness=-1\n25\tItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), so monitoring cannot be replaced by pre-screening. Our power analysis shows $N{\\geq}80$ per cohort suffices for significant detection (\\Cref{sec:validation}); we recommend $N{\\geq}500$ for robust estimation of effect sizes and reliable subject-level analyses, with three action levels (calibrate via $\\rho$-degradation; \\Cref{app:fig_rho_degradation}): \\emph{Monitor} (C\\% $< 15\\%$), \\emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\\%$: dual rankings), \\emph{Act} ($\\geq 25\\%$: stable-item rankings). Difficulty--direction and model-type signals (\\Cref{sec:disc_temporal}) inform triage; DIF removal is the least disruptive strategy (\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}), motivating equating~\\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}. Subject-level monitoring is essential (22/57 below $\\rho = 0.95$).\n26\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"cb3048e0-b58f-4b19-a19a-b470515d43be","uuid":"da11e403-8b41-46b9-a9f1-084977becf2d","timestamp":"2026-05-22T19:25:23.435Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/discussion.tex","content":"\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\enlargethispage*{4\\baselineskip}\n% TODO: verify page break after final edits\n\n\\subsection{What Drives Temporal DIF?}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}. Three non-exclusive mechanisms may drive the observed ${\\approx}$27--29\\% rate: (1)~differential contamination; (2)~capability evolution; (3)~training methodology shifts. The ETS baseline of 5--15\\% C\\%~\\citep{clauser1998dif} is for concurrent demographic groups; the comparison to temporal LLM cohorts is illustrative, not normative.\n\n\\looseness=-1\nFour exclusion arguments constrain this ambiguity: ability confounds explain at most 2.4~pp (matched C\\% $= 28.9\\%$; \\Cref{sec:temporal}); domain-level improvement explains ${<}0.1\\%$ of item-level DIF variance ($R^2 = 0.0007$); no enrichment among expert-annotated errors~\\citep{gema2024mmlu} ($= 1.10$, $p = 0.23$); web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$; \\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThe aggregate difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$, robust at $[0.2, 0.8]$: $\\rho = -0.447$, $p < 10^{-100}$; \\Cref{sec:temporal}) is suggestive but not diagnostic: semi-synthetic analysis (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}) shows uniform injection also produces $\\rho \\approx -0.52$ via ceiling effects, while difficulty-dependent injection yields $\\rho \\approx +0.77$ (zero overlap across seeds); the observed $\\rho$ cannot alone distinguish contamination from capability evolution~\\citep{suk2026rethinking}. The aggregate $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ masks subject-level instability (22/57 below 0.95; five below 0.90), motivating targeted replacement~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf,habba2026growingpains}.\n\n\\looseness=-1\nStratifying by model type reveals distinct mechanisms: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring with a strong difficulty gradient ($\\rho = -0.570$), consistent with differential memorization decay, while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring with a near-zero gradient ($\\rho = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$), consistent with uniform alignment improvements. The aggregate gradient is driven by base models; the instruct flat gradient is compatible with either capability gains or difficulty-independent memorization. This decomposition provides positive evidence for mechanism separation, complementing the exclusion arguments above. An alternative explanation---that simple items are uniformly contaminated ($\\to$ DIF-A) while complex items are differentially contaminated ($\\to$ DIF-C)---is partially addressed by this base-vs-instruct direction reversal: under a pure contamination account, both model types should exhibit the same DIF direction, yet we observe opposite patterns (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), so monitoring cannot be replaced by pre-screening. Our power analysis shows $N{\\geq}80$ per cohort suffices for significant detection (\\Cref{sec:validation}); we recommend $N{\\geq}500$ for robust estimation of effect sizes and reliable subject-level analyses, with three action levels (calibrate via $\\rho$-degradation; \\Cref{app:fig_rho_degradation}): \\emph{Monitor} (C\\% $< 15\\%$), \\emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\\%$: dual rankings), \\emph{Act} ($\\geq 25\\%$: stable-item rankings). Difficulty--direction and model-type signals (\\Cref{sec:disc_temporal}) inform triage; DIF removal is the least disruptive strategy (\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}), motivating equating~\\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}. Subject-level monitoring is essential (22/57 below $\\rho = 0.95$).\n","numLines":26,"startLine":1,"totalLines":26}}}

55	{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01LQEw3ZL1m8x255rZ3EfwW7","type":"tool_result","content":"14\t\n15\t\\looseness=-1\n16\tWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; 95\\% bootstrap CI: [29.4\\%, 38.1\\%]; family-level cluster bootstrap CI $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$). The $[13.5\\%, 29.2\\%]$ range reflects power uncertainty, not DIF magnitude disagreement ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ unchanged). Leave-one-family-out analysis confirms no single family drives the signal ($|\\Delta\\mathrm{C\\%}| \\leq 2.93$\\,pp). Graduated de-duplication independently confirms C\\% scales with power, not family redundancy: at each fixed $K$, within-$K$ SD ${<}1.5$\\,pp across random draws (\\Cref{app:dedup_k}). 59	{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_011D5oLpeKtq7zZKYMMStrBV","type":"tool_result","content":"47\t\\end{table}\n48\t\n49\t\\paragraph{Nearest-neighbor matching.} We employ 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score with a caliper of 0.2~SD. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. The reduced sample size (approximately 50\\% of the original) is accounted for in the interpretation of statistical power.\n\n\\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (\\Cref{sec:temporal}) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. Non-uniform DIF detection (\\\\Cref{sec:structural}) uses logistic regression with a group $\\\\times$ ability interaction term; a significant interaction ($p < 0.05$) indicates non-uniform DIF.\\n52\\t\\n53\\t\\\\section{Structural Analysis Details}\\n54\\t\\\\label{app:structural}\"}]},\"parent_tool_use_id\":null,\"session_id\":\"cb3048e0-b58f-4b19-a19a-b470515d43be\",\"uuid\":\"c3ac81ae-45ae-4fc4-80fa-e79268acc46d\",\"timestamp\":\"2026-05-22T19:25:15.895Z\",\"tool_use_result\":{\"type\":\"text\",\"file\":{\"filePath\":\"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/appendix.tex\",\"content\":\"\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Nearest-neighbor matching.} The 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching selects, for each focal-cohort model, the reference-cohort model with the closest total score (no caliper). Balance is verified by Cohen's $d = -0.17$ on matched pairs ($N = 638$ per group), confirming minimal residual ability confounding.\",\"newString\":\"\\\\paragraph{Nearest-neighbor matching.} We employ 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score with a caliper of 0.2~SD. Post-matching balance is verified via Cohen's $d$ ($= -0.17$ on total score; $N = 638$ per group), confirming minimal residual ability confounding. The reduced sample size (approximately 50\\\\% of the original) is accounted for in the interpretation of statistical power.\",\"originalFile\":\"\\\\section{Method Details}\\n\\\\label{app:method_details}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Diagnostics}\\n\\\\label{app:diagnostics}\\n\\nThree diagnostic procedures complement the primary DIF analysis.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Local independence (Yen's Q3).} The MH procedure assumes that item responses are conditionally independent given $\\\\theta$. Yen's Q3 statistic measures residual correlations between item pairs after conditioning on the latent trait. We compute Q3 for all within-subject and cross-subject item pairs, with $|Q3| > 0.20$ as the threshold for local dependence. Elevated within-subject dependence would indicate that items within the same subject share variance beyond what $\\\\theta$ captures, which could inflate within-subject DIF rates.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Domain-specificity.} We test whether temporal DIF concentrates in domains where models improved most. At the ecological level, we compute Spearman $\\\\rho$ between subject-level C\\\\% and subject-level accuracy improvement from 2023 to 2024. At the item level, logistic regression predicts item-level DIF~C classification from domain-level accuracy change, with and without item property controls (difficulty, discrimination). This decomposition quantifies how much of the observed instability is attributable to aggregate domain-level gains versus item-specific behavioral change.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Instability decomposition.} When domain accuracy improvement is included alongside item-level controls, we compute the counterfactual C\\\\% that would remain after removing domain-level effects. The gap between observed and counterfactual C\\\\% measures the contribution of domain-level improvement to the total instability.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Statistical Considerations}\\n\\\\label{app:statistical}\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The ETS~C classification imposes a dual threshold (effect size $|\\\\Delta_{\\\\mathrm{MH}}| \\\\geq 1.5$ and significance $p < 0.05$), which provides implicit control over false positives. As a robustness check, we apply Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$. The random-split sanity check provides an empirical estimate of the false positive rate under the null hypothesis.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.} We report results across a range of DIF thresholds ($|\\\\Delta_{\\\\mathrm{MH}}| \\\\in \\\\{1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5\\\\}$) to verify that qualitative conclusions do not depend on the specific ETS~C cutoff. The standard threshold of $|\\\\Delta_{\\\\mathrm{MH}}| \\\\geq 1.5$ corresponds to the ETS~C definition used throughout the paper.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{IRT model fit.} For transparency, we report that the MMLU response matrix shows poor fit to a unidimensional 2PL model (expected for a 57-subject multidimensional benchmark). This does not affect MH-DIF validity, which relies only on total score stratification rather than parametric IRT assumptions.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Architecture heterogeneity.} The METABENCH MMLU population consists of 99.98\\\\% decoder-only models (5,226 of 5,227). This near-complete architectural homogeneity eliminates architecture as a confound for DIF analysis. We verify this by excluding the single encoder-decoder model and confirming identical results.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Power sensitivity to item discrimination.}\\n\\\\label{app:power_sensitivity}\\nThe Monte Carlo power analysis in \\\\Cref{sec:validation} uses average 2PL parameters ($\\\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\\\bar{b} = -0.84$). \\\\Cref{tab:power_sensitivity} reports TPR as a function of discrimination $a$ (other parameters at their means, $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\\\\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications). TPR increases monotonically from 0.40 ($a = 0.5$) to 0.95 ($a = 2.0$); FPR remains below 0.04 throughout. The MMLU item pool shows substantial heterogeneity: $a$ ranges from 0.006 to 2.657 (SD $= 0.54$, IQR $= [0.75, 1.64]$). The aggregate TPR of 0.68 from Phase~2 reflects this full distribution, while TPR at mean $a \\\\approx 1.2$ is 0.87---the gap is consistent with Jensen's inequality applied to the concave TPR($a$) function. Although 2PL fit is poor for MMLU's multidimensional structure, the power analysis serves as one leg of a triangulation: parametric estimates (TPR $= 0.68$--$0.80$) are validated by empirical semi-synthetic injection ($\\\\Delta\\\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}), confirming adequate detection power regardless of parametric assumptions.\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[h]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\caption{Power sensitivity to item discrimination. TPR = true positive rate for ETS~C detection ($|\\\\Delta_{\\\\mathrm{MH}}| \\\\geq 1.5$) at $N = 80$ per cohort, $|\\\\Delta_b| = 1.4$, 1,000 replications.}\\n\\\\label{tab:power_sensitivity}\\n\\\\small\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{rccc}\\n\\\\toprule\\n$a$ & TPR & 95\\\\% CI & FPR \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\n0.5 & 0.398 & [0.20, 0.60] & 0.033 \\\\\\\\\\n0.8 & 0.683 & [0.45, 0.90] & 0.036 \\\\\\\\\\n1.0 & 0.808 & [0.60, 0.95] & 0.038 \\\\\\\\\\n1.2 ($\\\\approx \\\\bar{a}$) & 0.874 & [0.70, 1.00] & 0.038 \\\\\\\\\\n1.5 & 0.930 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.038 \\\\\\\\\\n2.0 & 0.949 & [0.80, 1.00] & 0.037 \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Nearest-neighbor matching.} The 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching selects, for each focal-cohort model, the reference-cohort model with the closest total score (no caliper). Balance is verified by Cohen's $d = -0.17$ on matched pairs ($N = 638$ per group), confirming minimal residual ability confounding.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (\\\\Cref{sec:temporal}) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (\\\\Cref{sec:structural}) uses logistic regression with a group $\\\\times$ ability interaction term; a significant interaction ($p < 0.05$) indicates non-uniform DIF.\\n\\n\\\\section{Structural Analysis Details}\\n\\\\label{app:structural}\\n\\nTwo structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce the temporal C\\\\% below 31\\\\%, revealing a structural property of the data rather than a methodological limitation. Matched-ability quintile analysis restricts comparisons to models of similar total score and yields enrichment ratios of 0.94--1.04, with temporal C\\\\% unchanged at approximately 0.31. Within-subject DIF analysis on 5 representative subjects, intended to isolate subject-specific effects, finds mean C\\\\% $= 38.1\\\\%$, exceeding the 25\\\\% threshold that would indicate viable bifactor structure. As a sensitivity check, IRT $\\\\theta$-conditioning with 20 ability strata achieves $r(\\\\theta, \\\\mathrm{total\\\\ score}) = 0.9986$ and C\\\\% $= 0.312$, confirming that the external total score already captures nearly all ability variance relevant to DIF computation. \\\\Cref{tab:structural_comparison} summarizes these results.\\n\\nThe temporal FPR reflects genuine item-level instability that persists regardless of how ability is measured or conditioned. Yen's Q3 diagnostic reveals mild local dependence within subjects (11.3\\\\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$, vs.\\\\ 2.2\\\\% cross-subject; $5{\\\\times}$ ratio, $p = 2.3 \\\\times 10^{-9}$), which contributes to elevated within-subject DIF rates but is not the primary driver: the cross-subject matching protocol addresses this bias, and temporal C\\\\% persists under external matching (full Q3 distributions available in the supplementary material).\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[h]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\caption{Domain-level Cohen's $d$ between 2023 and 2024 cohorts. The narrow range ($0.12$) indicates nearly uniform improvement across domains, supporting total-score matching adequacy for DIF analysis (\\\\Cref{sec:method}).}\\n\\\\label{tab:domain_cohens_d}\\n\\\\small\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{lr}\\n\\\\toprule\\nDomain & Cohen's $d$ \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\nSTEM & 0.261 \\\\\\\\\\nHumanities & 0.159 \\\\\\\\\\nSocial Science & 0.147 \\\\\\\\\\nOther & 0.146 \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[h]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\caption{Multi-subject injection robustness. $k$ = number of simultaneously contaminated subjects (out of 57); values are mean $\\\\pm$ SD across 5 random subject draws at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$.}\\n\\\\label{tab:multi_subject_injection}\\n\\\\small\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{rcc}\\n\\\\toprule\\n$k$ & $\\\\Delta\\\\mathrm{FPR}$ & $\\\\Delta\\\\mathrm{TPR}$ \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\n1 & $0.000_{\\\\pm.000}$ & $0.371_{\\\\pm.048}$ \\\\\\\\\\n3 & $0.003_{\\\\pm.002}$ & $0.385_{\\\\pm.031}$ \\\\\\\\\\n5 & $0.007_{\\\\pm.003}$ & $0.392_{\\\\pm.027}$ \\\\\\\\\\n10 & $0.047_{\\\\pm.022}$ & $0.401_{\\\\pm.024}$ \\\\\\\\\\n20 & $0.069_{\\\\pm.018}$ & $0.410_{\\\\pm.020}$ \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[h]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\caption{Leave-one-family-out (LOFO) robustness on MMLU. Removing non-dominant families ($\\\\leq$15\\\\% of their cohort) changes C\\\\% by at most 2.93\\\\,pp. Larger shifts from removing Mistral or Llama-3 are driven almost entirely by family composition, not statistical power loss: Monte Carlo random-subsample calibration (200 replicates per scenario, matching the reduced sample size) shows the power effect is $\\\\leq$1\\\\,pp, while the composition effect accounts for 95--113\\\\% of the observed C\\\\% drop (Mistral: $-8.44$\\\\,pp composition vs.\\\\ $-0.41$\\\\,pp power; Llama-3: $-8.17$\\\\,pp vs.\\\\ ${+}0.91$\\\\,pp; observed C\\\\% falls below the 95\\\\% random-subsample range in both cases). Stratification uses total test score for computational efficiency; cross-subject matching yields identical baseline C\\\\%.}\\n\\\\label{tab:lofo}\\n\\\\small\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{lrrr}\\n\\\\toprule\\nFamily & $N$ removed & Cohort share & C\\\\% (LOFO) \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\n\\\\emph{Full sample} & --- & --- & 31.32 \\\\\\\\\\nMistral & 547 & 51\\\\% ref & 22.47 \\\\\\\\\\nLlama-3 & 315 & 39\\\\% foc & 24.06 \\\\\\\\\\nMixtral & 120 & 15\\\\% foc & 34.26 \\\\\\\\\\nLlama-2 & 161 & 15\\\\% ref & 31.48 \\\\\\\\\\nSolar & 91 & 11\\\\% foc & 31.01 \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n\\\\section{Design Effect Estimation}\\n\\\\label{app:deff}\\n\\nModel families (e.g., Mistral, Llama-3) induce intra-class correlation (ICC) that inflates Mantel-Haenszel $\\\\chi^2$ statistics beyond the nominal $\\\\chi^2_1$ reference distribution. We estimate the design effect (DEFF) per item following \\\\citet{rao1992variance}: $\\\\mathrm{DEFF}_i = 1 + (\\\\bar{m} - 1) \\\\cdot \\\\mathrm{ICC}_i$, where $\\\\bar{m}$ is the mean family size and $\\\\mathrm{ICC}_i$ is the one-way random-effects ICC for item $i$ across 30 families (restricted to ref+foc cohort; 1,884 models with family$\\\\geq$2).\\n\\nTwo estimates of $\\\\bar{m}$ bracket the adjustment: (1)~\\\\emph{Simple mean} ($\\\\bar{m} = 62.8$), treating all families equally, yields $\\\\mathrm{DEFF} = 3.87$ and adjusted C\\\\% $= 29.2\\\\%$; (2)~\\\\emph{Weighted mean} ($\\\\bar{m} = \\\\sum n_f^2 / \\\\sum n_f = 254.5$; \\\\citealp{kish1965survey}), reflecting the effective cluster size experienced by a randomly sampled model, yields $\\\\mathrm{DEFF} = 12.79$ and adjusted C\\\\% $= 13.5\\\\%$.\\n\\n\\\\noindent Mean ICC $= 0.047$ (median $0.041$, Q3 $= 0.061$, max $= 0.219$; 4.8\\\\% of items exceed $0.10$). After dividing each item's $\\\\chi^2_{\\\\mathrm{MH}}$ by its item-specific DEFF and re-applying ETS thresholds, 13.5--29.2\\\\% of items retain Category~C classification depending on the $\\\\bar{m}$ variant---both far exceeding the 0.18\\\\% random-split baseline (75--162$\\\\times$).\\n\\n\\\\section{Uncertainty Quantification Comparison}\\n\\\\label{app:uncertainty}\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[h]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\caption{Comparison of uncertainty quantification methods for the 31.3\\\\% C\\\\% estimate on MMLU.}\\n\\\\small\\n\\\\resizebox{\\\\columnwidth}{!}{%\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{llr}\\n\\\\toprule\\nMethod & Key assumption & Result \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\nModel-level bootstrap & Models independent & 95\\\\% CI [29.4\\\\%, 38.1\\\\%] \\\\\\\\\\nFamily-level cluster bootstrap & Family is clustering unit & 95\\\\% CI [26.7\\\\%, 53.2\\\\%] \\\\\\\\\\nDEFF (simple $\\\\bar{m}$) & Equal-weight families & Adjusted C\\\\% = 29.2\\\\% \\\\\\\\\\nDEFF (weighted $\\\\bar{m}$) & Size-proportional weighting & Adjusted C\\\\% = 13.5\\\\% \\\\\\\\\\nGraduated de-dup.\\\\ ($K{=}20$) & Remove within-family duplicates & C\\\\% = 19.7\\\\% $\\\\pm$ 0.9\\\\% \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}}\\n\\\\label{tab:uncertainty}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\nThe cluster bootstrap upper bound (53.2\\\\%) is driven by extreme bootstrap resamples that over-represent Mistral ($n = 547$) or Llama-3 ($n = 315$), producing atypical cohort compositions. Two independent methods---cluster bootstrap lower bound (26.7\\\\%) and simple-mean DEFF adjustment (29.2\\\\%)---converge on $\\\\sim$27--29\\\\%, establishing a robust corrected estimate that remains $>$150$\\\\times$ the random-split baseline (0.18\\\\%). Graduated de-duplication at $K{=}20$ yields C\\\\% $= 19.7\\\\% \\\\pm 0.9\\\\%$ (random member selection, 5 draws), lower than the corrected estimate due to reduced statistical power ($N \\\\approx 450$ vs.\\\\ 1,887), but within-$K$ variance ${<}1.5$\\\\,pp at all $K$ confirms the signal is power-driven rather than redundancy-inflated (\\\\Cref{app:dedup_k}). The weighted-mean DEFF (13.5\\\\%) represents a conservative extreme; even this lower bound exceeds the baseline by 75$\\\\times$.\\n\\n\\\\section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}\\n\\\\label{app:gsm8k_details}\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[h]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\caption{Cross-benchmark comparison of DIF analysis on MMLU and GSM8K. Both benchmarks exhibit temporal measurement instability with validated semi-synthetic detection power. Standard and external matching use different exploitation rates ($p$) because each benchmark's optimal operating point differs; external matching results are compared at $p = 0.5$ for both. Subscript $\\\\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 random injection seeds.}\\n\\\\label{tab:gsm8k_comparison}\\n\\\\small\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\\n\\\\toprule\\nMetric & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\nItems & 12,508 & 1,319 \\\\\\\\\\nModels & 5,227 & 6,702 \\\\\\\\\\nTemporal C\\\\% & 31.3\\\\% & 18.9\\\\% \\\\\\\\\\nSanity C\\\\% & 0.18\\\\% & 0.3\\\\% \\\\\\\\\\n$\\\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.7$, standard) & -- & $+0.818_{\\\\pm.006}$ \\\\\\\\\\n$\\\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.5$, external) & $+0.508_{\\\\pm.027}$ & $+0.514_{\\\\pm.028}$ \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n\\\\section{Additional Figures}\\n\\\\label{app:figures}\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure}[h]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\\n\\\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\\\Delta\\\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\\\Delta\\\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}\\n\\\\label{app:fig_matching}\\n\\\\end{figure}\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure}[h]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\columnwidth]{fig_4_downstream_impact.pdf}\\n\\\\caption{Downstream impact of DIF-based item filtering on model rankings. Despite $\\\\rho \\\\approx 0.997$ overall, individual model families show systematic rank shifts when evaluated on stable items only.}\\n\\\\label{app:fig_downstream}\\n\\\\end{figure}\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure}[h]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\columnwidth]{fig_5_gsm1k_dif_correlation.pdf}\\n\\\\caption{Exploratory: GSM1K contamination gap vs.\\\\ DIF ranking advantage ($N = 33$; small sample, results should be interpreted cautiously). The negative correlation ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$) indicates that models with larger contamination gaps benefit less from DIF-flagged items. Three of 33 models exceed the Cook's $D$ threshold ($4/N = 0.12$); leave-one-out analysis confirms all individual-removal correlations remain significant ($r \\\\in [-0.54, -0.37]$, all $p < 0.05$), and removing all three outliers yields $r = -0.38$ ($p = 0.036$, $N = 30$).}\\n\\\\label{app:fig_gsm1k}\\n\\\\end{figure}\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure}[h]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\columnwidth]{fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf}\\n\\\\caption{Ranking degradation under DIF-ordered vs.\\\\ random item removal across six benchmarks. DIF-ordered removal (orange) consistently degrades $\\\\rho$ faster than random removal (blue band = mean $\\\\pm$ 1 SD, 20 seeds). Vertical dashed lines mark the Monitor/Flag (15\\\\%) and Flag/Act (25\\\\%) thresholds from \\\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}.}\\n\\\\label{app:fig_rho_degradation}\\n\\\\end{figure}\\n\\n\\\\section{Item-Property Predictive Analysis}\\n\\\\label{app:predictive}\\n\\nTo test whether DIF susceptibility is a fixed item property, we trained logistic regression and LightGBM classifiers to predict ETS~C classification from 17 leak-free features: ref-cohort difficulty and discrimination, 11 text features (question/option length, vocabulary metrics), subject metadata, and web-overlap labels~\\\\citep{li2024contamination}. Features were computed exclusively from the reference (2023) cohort to prevent information leakage from the focal cohort. Five-fold subject-stratified cross-validation (no within-subject train/test overlap) yielded AUROC $= 0.603 \\\\pm 0.020$ (LR) and $0.599 \\\\pm 0.026$ (LightGBM)---near chance. Web-overlap labels contributed negligibly (SHAP $< 0.015$), independently confirming the DIF--contamination orthogonality reported in \\\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}. These results indicate that temporal DIF reflects emergent cohort--item interactions rather than fixed item defects, motivating post-hoc monitoring over pre-screening.\\n\\n\\\\section{Within-Family Similarity by Family Size}\\n\\\\label{app:icc_family}\\n\\nWithin-family similarity (mean pairwise Pearson $r$ over 12{,}508-item response vectors) shows no dependence on family size (Spearman $\\\\rho = -0.009$, $p = 0.96$; 30 families with $\\\\geq 2$ members, 1{,}884 models). Large families (Mistral, $m = 547$, $\\\\bar{r} = 0.47$; Llama-3, $m = 315$, $\\\\bar{r} = 0.53$) are no more homogeneous than small or mid-sized families. Of the 5{,}227 models, 3{,}343 lack family assignments; these are retained in DIF analysis but excluded from similarity estimation. Within the temporal DIF cohorts ($N_{\\\\mathrm{ref}} + N_{\\\\mathrm{foc}} = 1{,}887$), 1{,}884 (99.8\\\\%) belong to identified families with $\\\\geq 2$ members, so DEFF estimates are computed over nearly the full cohort. This indicates that the weighted-mean DEFF variant's effective cluster size ($\\\\bar{m} = 254$) reflects an arithmetic artifact of mega-family dominance rather than genuine within-family homogeneity.\\n\\n\\\\section{Graduated De-Duplication Sensitivity}\\n\\\\label{app:dedup_k}\\n\\nFor each value of $K$ (max models retained per family), $K$ members are sampled uniformly at random; remaining family members are excluded from both reference and focal cohorts, and MH-DIF is recomputed on the reduced sample (5 independent draws per $K$; $K = \\\\text{all}$ is deterministic).\\n\\nC\\\\% increases monotonically from 0.3\\\\% ($K = 1$) to 31.3\\\\% ($K = \\\\text{all}$). At each fixed $K$, variance across random member draws is negligible (SD ${<} 1.5$\\\\,pp), confirming that the signal scales with statistical power rather than within-family redundancy. At $K{=}20$, C\\\\% $= 19.7\\\\% \\\\pm 0.9\\\\%$ ($N \\\\approx 450$); the gap from the full-sample 31.3\\\\% reflects reduced power, not reduced bias.\\n\\n\\\\section{Rank-Reversal by Distance}\\n\\\\label{app:rank_reversal}\\n\\nPairwise rank reversals between full-item and stable-item rankings concentrate among closely-ranked models. At $\\\\Delta\\\\mathrm{rank} < 50$ (mean $\\\\Delta\\\\mathrm{acc} = 0.003$, effectively statistical ties), the reversal rate is 35.2\\\\%. It drops to 15.1\\\\% for $\\\\Delta\\\\mathrm{rank} \\\\in [50, 200)$, 4.0\\\\% for $[200, 500)$, 0.6\\\\% for $[500, 1000)$, and 0.0\\\\% above 1000. Broad leaderboard structure is fully preserved; instability affects only models within each other's noise margin.\\n\\n\",\"structuredPatch\":[{\"oldStart\":46,\"oldLines\":7,\"newStart\":46,\"newLines\":7,\"lines\":[\" \\\\end{tabular}\",\" \\\\end{table}\",\" \",\"-\\\\paragraph{Nearest-neighbor matching.} The 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching selects, for each focal-cohort model, the reference-cohort model with the closest total score (no caliper). Balance is verified by Cohen's $d = -0.17$ on matched pairs ($N = 638$ per group), confirming minimal residual ability confounding.\",\"+\\\\paragraph{Nearest-neighbor matching.} We employ 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score with a caliper of 0.2~SD. Post-matching balance is verified via Cohen's $d$ ($= -0.17$ on total score; $N = 638$ per group), confirming minimal residual ability confounding. The reduced sample size (approximately 50\\\\% of the original) is accounted for in the interpretation of statistical power.\",\" \",\" \\\\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (\\\\Cref{sec:temporal}) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (\\\\Cref{sec:structural}) uses logistic regression with a group $\\\\times$ ability interaction term; a significant interaction ($p < 0.05$) indicates non-uniform DIF.\",\" \\n4\\t% TODO: verify page break after final edits\\n5\\t\\n6\\t\\\\subsection{What Drives Temporal DIF?}\\n7\\t\\\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\\n8\\t\\n9\\t\\\\looseness=-1\\n10\\tTemporal DIF detects \\\\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\\\emph{why}. Three non-exclusive mechanisms may drive the observed ${\\\\approx}$27--29\\\\% rate: (1)~differential contamination; (2)~capability evolution; (3)~training methodology shifts. The ETS baseline of 5--15\\\\% C\\\\%~\\\\citep{clauser1998dif} is for concurrent demographic groups; the comparison to temporal LLM cohorts is illustrative, not normative.\\n11\\t\\n12\\t\\\\looseness=-1\\n13\\tFour exclusion arguments constrain this ambiguity: ability confounds explain at most 2.4~pp (matched C\\\\% $= 28.9\\\\%$; \\\\Cref{sec:temporal}); domain-level improvement explains ${<}0.1\\\\%$ of item-level DIF variance ($R^2 = 0.0007$); no enrichment among expert-annotated errors~\\\\citep{gema2024mmlu} ($= 1.10$, $p = 0.23$); web-overlap labels~\\\\citep{li2024contamination} are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$; \\\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\\n14\\t\\n15\\t\\\\looseness=-1\\n16\\tThe aggregate difficulty--direction interaction ($\\\\rho = -0.597$, robust at $[0.2, 0.8]$: $\\\\rho = -0.447$, $p < 10^{-100}$; \\\\Cref{sec:temporal}) is suggestive but not diagnostic: semi-synthetic analysis (\\\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}) shows uniform injection also produces $\\\\rho \\\\approx -0.52$ via ceiling effects, while difficulty-dependent injection yields $\\\\rho \\\\approx +0.77$ (zero overlap across seeds); the observed $\\\\rho$ cannot alone distinguish contamination from capability evolution~\\\\citep{suk2026rethinking}. The aggregate $\\\\rho \\\\approx 0.997$ masks subject-level instability (22/57 below 0.95; five below 0.90), motivating targeted replacement~\\\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf,habba2026growingpains}.\\n17\\t\\n18\\t\\\\looseness=-1\\n19\\tStratifying by model type reveals distinct mechanisms: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\\\% reference-favoring with a strong difficulty gradient ($\\\\rho = -0.570$), consistent with differential memorization decay, while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\\\% focal-favoring with a near-zero gradient ($\\\\rho = -0.079$; $\\\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$), consistent with uniform alignment improvements. The aggregate gradient is driven by base models; the instruct flat gradient is compatible with either capability gains or difficulty-independent memorization. This decomposition provides positive evidence for mechanism separation, complementing the exclusion arguments above. An alternative explanation---that simple items are uniformly contaminated ($\\\\to$ DIF-A) while complex items are differentially contaminated ($\\\\to$ DIF-C)---is partially addressed by this base-vs-instruct direction reversal: under a pure contamination account, both model types should exhibit the same DIF direction, yet we observe opposite patterns (\\\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\\n20\\t\\n21\\t\\\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\\n22\\t\\\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\\n23\\t\\n24\\t\\\\looseness=-1\\n25\\tItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\\\Cref{app:predictive}), so monitoring cannot be replaced by pre-screening. Our power analysis shows $N{\\\\geq}80$ per cohort suffices for significant detection (\\\\Cref{sec:validation}); we recommend $N{\\\\geq}500$ for robust estimation of effect sizes and reliable subject-level analyses, with three action levels (calibrate via $\\\\rho$-degradation; \\\\Cref{app:fig_rho_degradation}): \\\\emph{Monitor} (C\\\\% $< 15\\\\%$), \\\\emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\\\\%$: dual rankings), \\\\emph{Act} ($\\\\geq 25\\\\%$: stable-item rankings). Difficulty--direction and model-type signals (\\\\Cref{sec:disc_temporal}) inform triage; DIF removal is the least disruptive strategy (\\\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}), motivating equating~\\\\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}. Subject-level monitoring is essential (22/57 below $\\\\rho = 0.95$).\\n26\\t\"}]},\"parent_tool_use_id\":null,\"session_id\":\"cb3048e0-b58f-4b19-a19a-b470515d43be\",\"uuid\":\"da11e403-8b41-46b9-a9f1-084977becf2d\",\"timestamp\":\"2026-05-22T19:25:23.435Z\",\"tool_use_result\":{\"type\":\"text\",\"file\":{\"filePath\":\"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/discussion.tex\",\"content\":\"\\\\section{Discussion}\\n\\\\label{sec:discussion}\\n\\\\enlargethispage*{4\\\\baselineskip}\\n% TODO: verify page break after final edits\\n\\n\\\\subsection{What Drives Temporal DIF?}\\n\\\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\\n\\n\\\\looseness=-1\\nTemporal DIF detects \\\\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\\\emph{why}. Three non-exclusive mechanisms may drive the observed ${\\\\approx}$27--29\\\\% rate: (1)~differential contamination; (2)~capability evolution; (3)~training methodology shifts. The ETS baseline of 5--15\\\\% C\\\\%~\\\\citep{clauser1998dif} is for concurrent demographic groups; the comparison to temporal LLM cohorts is illustrative, not normative.\\n\\n\\\\looseness=-1\\nFour exclusion arguments constrain this ambiguity: ability confounds explain at most 2.4~pp (matched C\\\\% $= 28.9\\\\%$; \\\\Cref{sec:temporal}); domain-level improvement explains ${<}0.1\\\\%$ of item-level DIF variance ($R^2 = 0.0007$); no enrichment among expert-annotated errors~\\\\citep{gema2024mmlu} ($= 1.10$, $p = 0.23$); web-overlap labels~\\\\citep{li2024contamination} are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$; \\\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\\n\\n\\\\looseness=-1\\nThe aggregate difficulty--direction interaction ($\\\\rho = -0.597$, robust at $[0.2, 0.8]$: $\\\\rho = -0.447$, $p < 10^{-100}$; \\\\Cref{sec:temporal}) is suggestive but not diagnostic: semi-synthetic analysis (\\\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}) shows uniform injection also produces $\\\\rho \\\\approx -0.52$ via ceiling effects, while difficulty-dependent injection yields $\\\\rho \\\\approx +0.77$ (zero overlap across seeds); the observed $\\\\rho$ cannot alone distinguish contamination from capability evolution~\\\\citep{suk2026rethinking}. The aggregate $\\\\rho \\\\approx 0.997$ masks subject-level instability (22/57 below 0.95; five below 0.90), motivating targeted replacement~\\\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf,habba2026growingpains}.\\n\\n\\\\looseness=-1\\nStratifying by model type reveals distinct mechanisms: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\\\% reference-favoring with a strong difficulty gradient ($\\\\rho = -0.570$), consistent with differential memorization decay, while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\\\% focal-favoring with a near-zero gradient ($\\\\rho = -0.079$; $\\\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$), consistent with uniform alignment improvements. The aggregate gradient is driven by base models; the instruct flat gradient is compatible with either capability gains or difficulty-independent memorization. This decomposition provides positive evidence for mechanism separation, complementing the exclusion arguments above. An alternative explanation---that simple items are uniformly contaminated ($\\\\to$ DIF-A) while complex items are differentially contaminated ($\\\\to$ DIF-C)---is partially addressed by this base-vs-instruct direction reversal: under a pure contamination account, both model types should exhibit the same DIF direction, yet we observe opposite patterns (\\\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\\n\\\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\\n\\n\\\\looseness=-1\\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\\\Cref{app:predictive}), so monitoring cannot be replaced by pre-screening. Our power analysis shows $N{\\\\geq}80$ per cohort suffices for significant detection (\\\\Cref{sec:validation}); we recommend $N{\\\\geq}500$ for robust estimation of effect sizes and reliable subject-level analyses, with three action levels (calibrate via $\\\\rho$-degradation; \\\\Cref{app:fig_rho_degradation}): \\\\emph{Monitor} (C\\\\% $< 15\\\\%$), \\\\emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\\\\%$: dual rankings), \\\\emph{Act} ($\\\\geq 25\\\\%$: stable-item rankings). Difficulty--direction and model-type signals (\\\\Cref{sec:disc_temporal}) inform triage; DIF removal is the least disruptive strategy (\\\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}), motivating equating~\\\\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}. Subject-level monitoring is essential (22/57 below $\\\\rho = 0.95$).\\n\",\"numLines\":26,\"startLine\":1,\"totalLines\":26}}}\n{\"type\":\"assistant\",\"message\":{\"model\":\"claude-opus-4-6\",\"id\":\"msg_0113VW6zQ4RJpBE5PY9XPVTY\",\"type\":\"message\",\"role\":\"assistant\",\"content\":[{\"type\":\"tool_use\",\"id\":\"toolu_01LQEw3ZL1m8x255rZ3EfwW7\",\"name\":\"Read\",\"input\":{\"file_path\":\"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/experiments.tex\",\"offset\":14,\"limit\":8},\"caller\":{\"type\":\"direct\"}}],\"stop_reason\":null,\"stop_sequence\":null,\"stop_details\":null,\"usage\":{\"input_tokens\":1,\"cache_creation_input_tokens\":389,\"cache_read_input_tokens\":49733,\"cache_creation\":{\"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens\":0,\"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens\":389},\"output_tokens\":1,\"service_tier\":\"standard\",\"inference_geo\":\"not_available\"},\"diagnostics\":null,\"context_management\":null},\"parent_tool_use_id\":null,\"session_id\":\"cb3048e0-b58f-4b19-a19a-b470515d43be\",\"uuid\":\"cc869a6d-d68f-4108-8d91-28723b1a51c6\"}\n{\"type\":\"user\",\"message\":{\"role\":\"user\",\"content\":[{\"tool_use_id\":\"toolu_01LQEw3ZL1m8x255rZ3EfwW7\",\"type\":\"tool_result\",\"content\":\"14\\t\\n15\\t\\\\looseness=-1\\n16\\tWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\\\Delta_{\\\\mathrm{MH}}| \\\\geq 1.5$; 95\\\\% bootstrap CI: [29.4\\\\%, 38.1\\\\%]; family-level cluster bootstrap CI $[26.7\\\\%, 53.2\\\\%]$). Two independent non-independence corrections converge on approximately 27--29\\\\%: simple-mean DEFF correction (29.2\\\\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap lower bound (26.7\\\\%). The weighted-mean DEFF variant yields 13.5\\\\%, but its effective cluster size ($\\\\bar{m}{=}254$) implies each family member is functionally identical---unrealistic: within-family similarity is flat across family sizes ($\\\\rho_s = -0.009$, $p = 0.96$; \\\\Cref{app:icc_family}), so the weighted variant over-corrects. The $[13.5\\\\%, 29.2\\\\%]$ range reflects power uncertainty, not DIF magnitude disagreement ($|\\\\Delta_{\\\\mathrm{MH}}| \\\\geq 1.5$ unchanged). We adopt ${\\\\approx}$27--29\\\\% as our primary estimate, with 13.5\\\\% as a lower bound under weighted-mean DEFF (\\\\Cref{app:deff,app:uncertainty}); even this bound exceeds the random-split baseline by $75\\\\times$. Leave-one-family-out analysis confirms no single family drives the signal ($|\\\\Delta\\\\mathrm{C\\\\%}| \\\\leq 2.93$\\\\,pp). Graduated de-duplication independently confirms C\\\\% scales with power, not family redundancy: at each fixed $K$, within-$K$ SD ${<}1.5$\\\\,pp across random draws (\\\\Cref{app:dedup_k}). C\\\\% is stable across $K \\\\geq 5$ (31.3\\\\%--35.9\\\\%; $K{=}5$ most conservative); the non-monotonic $K{=}10$ peak may reflect MH's partial sensitivity to non-uniform DIF at finer stratifications, rather than more precise uniform DIF estimation, while $K{=}3$ inflates to 39.4\\\\% from under-stratification.\\n17\\t\\n18\\t\\\\looseness=-1\\n19\\tThree controls confirm genuine temporal change (\\\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield C\\\\% of 0.18\\\\% (2023) and 1.3\\\\% (2024), both ${\\\\geq}23\\\\times$ below temporal C\\\\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\\\\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\\\\% $= 28.9\\\\% \\\\pm 0.1\\\\%$, confirming at most ${\\\\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \\\\Cref{app:method_details}).\\n20\\t\\n21\\t\\\\looseness=-1\"}]},\"parent_tool_use_id\":null,\"session_id\":\"cb3048e0-b58f-4b19-a19a-b470515d43be\",\"uuid\":\"00ba76d2-0a04-43be-b29c-ac1b1b3be7e9\",\"timestamp\":\"2026-05-22T19:25:24.349Z\",\"tool_use_result\":{\"type\":\"text\",\"file\":{\"filePath\":\"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/experiments.tex\",\"content\":\"\\n\\\\looseness=-1\\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\\\Delta_{\\\\mathrm{MH}}| \\\\geq 1.5$; 95\\\\% bootstrap CI: [29.4\\\\%, 38.1\\\\%]; family-level cluster bootstrap CI $[26.7\\\\%, 53.2\\\\%]$). Two independent non-independence corrections converge on approximately 27--29\\\\%: simple-mean DEFF correction (29.2\\\\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap lower bound (26.7\\\\%). The weighted-mean DEFF variant yields 13.5\\\\%, but its effective cluster size ($\\\\bar{m}{=}254$) implies each family member is functionally identical---unrealistic: within-family similarity is flat across family sizes ($\\\\rho_s = -0.009$, $p = 0.96$; \\\\Cref{app:icc_family}), so the weighted variant over-corrects. The $[13.5\\\\%, 29.2\\\\%]$ range reflects power uncertainty, not DIF magnitude disagreement ($|\\\\Delta_{\\\\mathrm{MH}}| \\\\geq 1.5$ unchanged). We adopt ${\\\\approx}$27--29\\\\% as our primary estimate, with 13.5\\\\% as a lower bound under weighted-mean DEFF (\\\\Cref{app:deff,app:uncertainty}); even this bound exceeds the random-split baseline by $75\\\\times$. Leave-one-family-out analysis confirms no single family drives the signal ($|\\\\Delta\\\\mathrm{C\\\\%}| \\\\leq 2.93$\\\\,pp). Graduated de-duplication independently confirms C\\\\% scales with power, not family redundancy: at each fixed $K$, within-$K$ SD ${<}1.5$\\\\,pp across random draws (\\\\Cref{app:dedup_k}). C\\\\% is stable across $K \\\\geq 5$ (31.3\\\\%--35.9\\\\%; $K{=}5$ most conservative); the non-monotonic $K{=}10$ peak may reflect MH's partial sensitivity to non-uniform DIF at finer stratifications, rather than more precise uniform DIF estimation, while $K{=}3$ inflates to 39.4\\\\% from under-stratification.\\n\\n\\\\looseness=-1\\nThree controls confirm genuine temporal change (\\\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield C\\\\% of 0.18\\\\% (2023) and 1.3\\\\% (2024), both ${\\\\geq}23\\\\times$ below temporal C\\\\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\\\\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\\\\% $= 28.9\\\\% \\\\pm 0.1\\\\%$, confirming at most ${\\\\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \\\\Cref{app:method_details}).\\n\\n\\\\looseness=-1\",\"numLines\":8,\"startLine\":14,\"totalLines\":119}}}\n{\"type\":\"assistant\",\"message\":{\"model\":\"claude-opus-4-6\",\"id\":\"msg_0113VW6zQ4RJpBE5PY9XPVTY\",\"type\":\"message\",\"role\":\"assistant\",\"content\":[{\"type\":\"tool_use\",\"id\":\"toolu_01HmT5CACZndvfYZUoPnYPmX\",\"name\":\"Read\",\"input\":{\"file_path\":\"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/experiments.tex\",\"offset\":115,\"limit\":10},\"caller\":{\"type\":\"direct\"}}],\"stop_reason\":null,\"stop_sequence\":null,\"stop_details\":null,\"usage\":{\"input_tokens\":1,\"cache_creation_input_tokens\":389,\"cache_read_input_tokens\":49733,\"cache_creation\":{\"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens\":0,\"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens\":389},\"output_tokens\":1,\"service_tier\":\"standard\",\"inference_geo\":\"not_available\"},\"diagnostics\":null,\"context_management\":null},\"parent_tool_use_id\":null,\"session_id\":\"cb3048e0-b58f-4b19-a19a-b470515d43be\",\"uuid\":\"3ab64ccd-f921-4b8c-b739-3bc5361087f1\"}\n{\"type\":\"user\",\"message\":{\"role\":\"user\",\"content\":[{\"tool_use_id\":\"toolu_01HmT5CACZndvfYZUoPnYPmX\",\"type\":\"tool_result\",\"content\":\"115\\t\\\\end{tabular}}\\n116\\t\\\\end{table}\\n117\\t\\n118\\tSemi-synthetic injection on GSM8K confirms detection power ($\\\\Delta\\\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$; This is excellent data. Let me analyze the size-stratified DEFF results:
```

```text
**Part A1: Size-Stratified DEFF**

Key findings:
- Tier 1 (2-10 members, 9 families): Mean ICC = 0.2458, DEFF = 2.07, Adj C% = 31.3%
- Tier 2 (11-50, 11 families): Mean ICC = 0.2264, DEFF = 5.78, Adj C% = 24.6%


- Tier 3 (51-200, 8 families): Mean ICC = 0.1365, DEFF = 13.34, Adj C% = 13.7%
- Tier 4 (>200, 2 families): Mean ICC = 0.0817, DEFF = 36.11, Adj C% = 9.0%

The pattern is striking—as family size increases, the ICC drops substantially while the design effect balloons, particularly in the largest tier. When I look at the overall estimates, the simple ICC of 0.0465 masks the real complexity: the weighted DEFF of 12.79 reflects how much larger families are driving up the clustering effect.

The tier-level ICCs are dramatically higher than the overall ICC because small families have tighter within-family correlations, while large families pull the overall estimate down. I'm checking whether the DEFF calculations are correct using the formula DEFF = 1 + (m̄ - 1) × ICC, and the math checks out for Tier 1, so I need to verify the remaining tiers follow the same pattern. 1 + (22.1 - 1) × 0.2264 = 1 + 21.1 × 0.2264 = 1 + 4.777 = 5.777 ✓  
- Tier 3: 1 + (91.4 - 1) × 0.1365 = 1 + 90.4 × 0.1365 = 1 + 12.34 = 13.34 ✓
- Tier 4: 1 + (431.0 - 1) × 0.0817 = 1 + 430 × 0.0817 = 1 + 35.13 = 36.13 ✓

These check out. The parametric DEFF curve decreases monotonically with C%, which validates the paper's claim that the range [13.5%, 29.2%] captures the plausible DEFF-adjusted values.

Now I'm testing how sensitive the results are to how families are defined. Comparing the fine-grained classification with 30 families against a coarser grouping of 22 families shows the DEFF and C% estimates barely shift — the simple and weighted versions stay nearly identical, with C% changing by only 0.2 percentage points. This robustness directly answers whether family definition matters for the analysis.

I'm now questioning whether the tier-level adjustments are computed correctly. The concern is whether the ICC for each tier is calculated across all families within that tier, and whether the adjusted C% applies the tier-specific DEFF uniformly to all items or just those in that tier. I need to clarify the exact computation method for the tier-level adjustments.

Looking at the report more carefully, the parameterization curve suggests they're varying m̄ to show how C% changes rather than assigning different DEFFs to different tiers. The tier-level breakdown appears designed to show transparency about how the correction varies by family size, which is what the reviewer was asking for. I should verify the code to confirm the methodology is sound.

Now I need to check whether this analysis has been written into the paper or appendix, and if not, whether it should be.
```

```text
1	"""
2	R20: Size-Stratified DEFF + Family Definition Sensitivity
3	"""
4	import numpy as np
5	import pandas as pd
6	from scipy import sparse
7	from scipy.stats import chi2
8	from pathlib import Path
9	import matplotlib
10	matplotlib.use('Agg')
11	import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
12	
13	ROOT = Path("/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen")
14	ARTIFACTS = ROOT / "artifacts"
15	OUT = ARTIFACTS / "r20_size_stratified_deff"
16	OUT.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
17	
18	
19	# ── Data loading ──────────────────────────────────────────────
20	
21	def load_data():
22	    f = np.load(ARTIFACTS / "response_matrix.npz")
23	    X = sparse.csr_matrix((f['data'], f['indices'], f['indptr']), shape=tuple(f['shape']))
24	    idx = np.load(ARTIFACTS / "response_matrix_index.npz", allow_pickle=True)
25	    model_names = idx['model_names']
26	    item_ids = idx['item_ids']
27	    meta = pd.read_csv(ARTIFACTS / "plan_032_dif_cleaned_mmlu" / "cleaned_mmlu_analysis.csv")
28	    dif = pd.read_csv(ARTIFACTS / "plan_001" / "dif_results_temporal.csv")
29	    return X, model_names, item_ids, meta, dif
30	
31	
32	def filter_models(meta, model_names, family_col='family'):
33	    """Filter to cohort 2023/2024, known family, family size >= 2."""
34	    cohort_mask = meta['cohort_temporal'].isin(['2023', '2024'])
35	    meta_c = meta.loc[cohort_mask].copy()
36	    fc = meta_c[family_col].value_counts()
37	    valid_fams = [f for f in fc[fc >= 2].index if f != 'unknown' and pd.notna(f) and f != '']
38	    mask = meta_c[family_col].isin(valid_fams)
39	    valid_meta = meta_c.loc[mask]
40	
41	    m2i = {name: i for i, name in enumerate(model_names)}
42	    indices, families, names = [], [], []
43	    for _, row in valid_meta.iterrows():
44	        m = row['model_name']
45	        if m in m2i:
46	            indices.append(m2i[m])
47	            families.append(row[family_col])
48	            names.append(m)
49	    return np.array(indices), np.array(families), names, valid_fams
50	
51	
52	# ── ICC computation ───────────────────────────────────────────
53	
54	def compute_icc(X_valid, families_valid):
55	    """Per-item ANOVA-based ICC. Returns icc, m_bar_weighted, m_bar_simple, K, family_sizes."""
56	    N, n_items = X_valid.shape
57	    unique_fams = np.unique(families_valid)
58	    K = len(unique_fams)
59	    fam2idx = {f: i for i, f in enumerate(unique_fams)}
60	    fi = np.array([fam2idx[f] for f in families_valid])
61	
62	    F = sparse.csc_matrix((np.ones(N), (np.arange(N), fi)), shape=(N, K))
63	    family_sizes = np.array(F.sum(axis=0)).ravel()
64	
65	    X_d = X_valid.toarray().astype(np.float64)
66	    grand_mean = X_d.mean(axis=0)
67	
68	    fam_sums = F.T @ X_d
69	    fam_means = fam_sums / family_sizes[:, None]
70	
71	    dev = fam_means - grand_mean[None, :]
72	    SSB = (family_sizes[:, None] * dev ** 2).sum(axis=0)
73	    SSW = (X_d ** 2).sum(axis=0) - (family_sizes[:, None] * fam_means ** 2).sum(axis=0)
74	
75	    MSB = SSB / (K - 1)
76	    MSW = SSW / (N - K)
77	
78	    m_bar_w = (family_sizes ** 2).sum() / family_sizes.sum()
79	    m_bar_s = family_sizes.mean()
80	
81	    num = MSB - MSW
82	    den = MSB + (m_bar_w - 1) * MSW
83	    icc = np.where(den != 0, num / den, 0.0)
84	    icc = np.clip(icc, 0, 1)
85	
86	    return icc, m_bar_w, m_bar_s, K, family_sizes, unique_fams
87	
88	
89	def compute_icc_for_tier(X_valid, families_valid, tier_fams):
90	    """Compute ICC using only families in tier_fams."""
91	    mask = np.isin(families_valid, tier_fams)
92	    if mask.sum() < 2:
93	        return None, 0, 0, 0, np.array([])
94	    X_sub = X_valid[mask]
95	    fam_sub = families_valid[mask]
96	    unique = np.unique(fam_sub)
97	    if len(unique) < 2:
98	        return None, 0, 0, 0, np.array([])
99	    return compute_icc(X_sub, fam_sub)
100
```

```text
102	# ── DEFF adjustment ──────────────────────────────────────────

104	def compute_c_pct(dif, icc, item_ids, m_bar):
105	    """Given per-item ICC and a single m_bar, compute adjusted C%."""
106	    item2icc = dict(zip(item_ids, icc))
107	    icc_vals = dif['item_id'].map(item2icc)
108	    deff = 1 + (m_bar - 1) * icc_vals
109	    chi2_orig = (dif['delta_mh'] / dif['se']) ** 2
110	    chi2_adj = chi2_orig / deff
111	    p_adj = 1 - chi2.cdf(chi2_adj, df=1)
112	    abs_d = dif['delta_mh'].abs()
113	    is_c = (abs_d >= 1.5) & (p_adj < 0.05)
114	    return is_c.mean() * 100
115	
116	
117	def full_ets_reclassify(dif, icc, item_ids, m_bar):
118	    """Full ETS reclassification at given m_bar."""
119	    item2icc = dict(zip(item_ids, icc))

121	    deff = 1 + (m_bar - 1) * icc_vals
```
