128	    ets[(abs_d >= 1.5) & (p_adj < 0.05)] = 'C'

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I understand the methodology:

**Part A1 Tier-Stratified DEFF:**
1. For each tier (family size range), it computes ICC using only models from families in that tier
2. The tier ICC is computed via the standard ANOVA-based ICC formula on the subset
3. Then it uses `compute_c_pct()` to apply the tier-specific ICC with the tier's mean m̄ to ALL items
4. This gives the "what if all families were this size with this ICC" adjusted C%

This is methodologically sound. The key insight: small families have higher ICC (0.2458) but smaller m̄ (5.3), so their DEFF is modest (2.07). 

Large families have lower ICC (0.0817) but much larger m̄ (431), so their DEFF is huge (36.11). The overall ICC (0.0465) is much lower than any tier ICC because when computing across all 30 families, the between-family variance grows substantially with the diversity of family sizes, which dilutes the within-family ICC. Actually, I need to reconsider—the ICC is computed per item, decomposing variance into between-family and within-family components, and including families of different sizes increases the between-family variance...

Looking at how the ICC is actually calculated, when only small families are included, there are fewer models but high within-family similarity since these are closely related models. When all families are included, the large families like Mistral and Llama-3 contribute many models with greater internal diversity, which drags down the overall ICC. So the aggregate ICC of 0.047 is really just an artifact of mixing small families with high ICC against large families with low ICC.

The adjustment for clustering effects in Tier 1 shows that DEFF of 2.07 is modest enough that dividing χ² by 2 doesn't shift many classifications from significant to non-significant when the effects are large, which is reassuring. But in Tier 4, the adjustment is much more dramatic because the two mega-families drive substantial DEFF correction, though with only 2 families in that tier, the ICC estimate becomes less stable.

Now I'm checking how sensitive these results are to how families are defined, comparing the fine-grained classification with 30 families against a coarser grouping of 22 families.

The C% values barely budge between the two definitions — simple C% shifts by just 0.2 percentage points and weighted C% by 0.3 percentage points in the opposite direction. This directly confirms that the family definition doesn't materially affect the conclusions, which is exactly what Q1 was asking.

The methodology checks out across the board: the DEFF stratification by tier addresses the clustering concern, the ICC calculations are properly computed using ANOVA, and the per-item DEFF application is correct. But I'm noticing something odd — the tier-level ICCs range from 0.08 to 0.25, which is 2 to 5 times higher than the overall ICC of 0.047, and I need to understand why that discrepancy exists.

There's also a practical issue with Tier 4: it only contains 2 families, so any ICC estimate from that tier is going to be unstable and unreliable. That limitation should be flagged. I should also check whether this experiment was registered — I don't see a new entry in the registry, so I need to clarify that before finalizing my findings.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TUX1ruVSabSD2T1gxZ9M3b`</summary>

```text
49	\paragraph{Nearest-neighbor matching.} The 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching selects, for each focal-cohort model, the reference-cohort model with the closest total score (caliper of 0.02 on normalized accuracy, approximately 0.14~SD). Balance is verified by Cohen's $d = -0.17$ on matched pairs ($N = 638$ per group), confirming minimal residual ability confounding.
50	
51	\paragraph{Logistic regression specification.} The Simpson's paradox analysis (\Cref{sec:temporal}) uses binary logistic regression with DIF-C classification as the dependent variable, domain-level accuracy change (2024 minus 2023 mean accuracy) as the primary predictor, and item difficulty (proportion correct) and IRT discrimination (point-biserial correlation) as controls. The interaction between domain accuracy change and item difficulty is included. Non-uniform DIF detection (\Cref{sec:structural}) uses logistic regression with a group $\times$ ability interaction term; a significant interaction ($p < 0.05$) indicates non-uniform DIF.
```

</details>

122	
123	\paragraph{Size-stratified DEFF.} The aggregate ICC masks a size--ICC inverse relationship: smaller families exhibit higher within-family similarity (\Cref{tab:deff_stratified}). Tier-level ICC ranges from 0.246 (2--10 members) to 0.082 ({>}200 members), reflecting greater heterogeneity in mega-families. At small family sizes, DEFF correction has negligible impact (Adj.\ C\% $= 31.3\%$); the conservative 13.5\% bound is driven entirely by the two mega-families (Mistral, Llama-3). Family definition sensitivity is minimal: merging sub-generations (e.g., Llama-2 + Llama-3) into coarse families (22 families) changes C\% by $\leq$0.3~pp.
124	
125	\begin{table}[h]
126	\centering
127	\caption{Size-stratified DEFF analysis. ICC and DEFF computed per tier; Adj.\ C\% applies tier-specific DEFF to all items.}
128	\label{tab:deff_stratified}
129	\small
130	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrc}
131	\toprule
132	Tier & Families & $\bar{m}$ & ICC & DEFF & Adj.\ C\% \\
133	\midrule
134	2--10 & 9 & 5.3 & 0.246 & 2.07 & 31.3\% \\
135	11--50 & 11 & 22.1 & 0.226 & 5.78 & 24.6\% \\
136	51--200 & 8 & 91.4 & 0.137 & 13.34 & 13.7\% \\
137	${>}200$ & 2 & 431.0 & 0.082 & 36.11 & 9.0\% \\
138	\midrule
139	Overall & 30 & 62.8 & 0.047 & 3.87 & 29.2\% \\
140	\bottomrule
141	\end{tabular}
142	\end{table}

_source line 388 · 2026-05-22T20:40:21.036Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   I am the independent Research Reviewer (Loop #2) for the `irt_equated_bench_regen` project — a paper applying MH-DIF psychometric analysis to LLM benchmarks (5,227 MMLU models × 12,508 items), targeting EMNLP 2026 via ARR (DDL 5/25 AoE). My role is read-only verification: audit experiment conclusions, verify paper data consistency, and report findings via `reviewer_post_to_director`. Key tasks from handoff: (1) track "ruling out ceiling effects" fix in discussion.tex, (2) pre-submission audit, (3) verify R20 Must-Fix items, (4) review Worker outputs (reference_verification, text_edits, size-stratified DEFF), (5) review ROADMAP updates.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Mantel-Haenszel DIF (MH-DIF) with ETS C classification (|Δ_MH| ≥ 1.5)
   - Design Effect (DEFF) = 1 + (m̄ - 1) × ICC for non-independence correction
   - Size-stratified DEFF: tier-level ICC varies inversely with family size (0.246 for small → 0.082 for mega)
   - Temporal measurement instability: ~27-29% MMLU items after DEFF correction
   - Web-overlap labels (Li et al.) orthogonal to DIF flags (Jaccard=0.206)
   - Base-vs-instruct decomposition (χ²=562.7) for mechanism separation
   - Semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching (ΔFPR=0 guarantee)
   - ρ-gradient: difficulty-direction interaction (ρ=-0.597) is "suggestive but not diagnostic"
   - Cook's D outlier analysis for GSM1K correlation (Pearson r=-0.478 full, r=-0.384 after removing 3 outliers)
   - Family definition sensitivity: fine-grained (30 families) vs coarse (22 families) yields ±0.3 pp C% change

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - `paper/sections/discussion.tex`
     - Central to multiple reviews: "ruling out ceiling effects" fix verified at line 15, base-vs-instruct decomposition at line 18 (N=1,409 base, N=478 instruct, χ²=562.7), ≥500 threshold at line 24
     - Worker attempted but unpersisted changes: Q3 alternative explanation (B1), enlargethispage 20→4 (B6), TODO comment
   
   - `paper/sections/experiments.tex`
     - Lines 16: temporal DIF data (31.3%, bootstrap CI, DEFF corrections ≈27-29%)
     - Line 118: GSM1K analysis — currently "r = -0.478, p = 0.005", Worker attempted to change to exploratory framing with "Spearman r = -0.38" (data issue: actually Pearson)
     - Line 16: K=10 non-monotonic peak explanation — Worker attempted NUDIF caveat change (B3)
   
   - `paper/sections/appendix.tex`
     - Line 49: Matching caliper — FIXED in a407e0a to "caliper of 0.02 on normalized accuracy, approximately 0.14 SD" (was "no caliper", code shows caliper=0.02)
     - Lines 122-142: NEW size-stratified DEFF table (tab:deff_stratified) added in a407e0a
     - Line 205 (now "Within-Family Similarity by Family Size"): Title renamed from "ICC by Family Size" in a928c56
     - Lines 114-121: DEFF estimation section with Mean ICC=0.047
   
   - `paper/sections/abstract.tex` — "5,227 MMLU models" (W-N3 fix verified)
   - `paper/sections/introduction.tex` — "Orthogonal constructs" contribution (line 22), χ²=562.7 (line 26)
   - `paper/sections/conclusion.tex` — Data consistent (17.9-31.3%, Jaccard=0.206, ρ≈0.997)
   - `paper/sections/limitations.tex` — 1 paragraph, 3 points, EMNLP compliant
   
   - `artifacts/r20_size_stratified_deff/size_stratified_deff.py`
     - ICC computation via ANOVA: `compute_icc()` (lines 54-86), `compute_icc_for_tier()` (lines 89-99)
     - DEFF adjustment: `compute_c_pct()` divides each item's χ² by item-specific DEFF (lines 104-114)
     - Coarse family mapping: COARSE_MAP dict (lines 142-157)
     - Tier definitions: 2-10, 11-50, 51-200, >200 (lines 194-198)
   
   - `artifacts/r20_size_stratified_deff/size_stratified_deff_report.md` — Full results table
   - `artifacts/r14_sf9_gsm1k_cooks/cooks_d_report.md` — After removing 3 outliers: Pearson r=-0.384, p=0.0364, N=30
   - `artifacts/plan_027_temporal_overlap/plan_027_temporal_overlap.py` — Lines 359-383: `nn_match()` with `caliper=0.02`

4. Errors and fixes:
   - **Layout checker false positives**: 6 critical findings from `check_figures_and_layout` were all false positives — tool doesn't support \Cref comma syntax, \section*, or graphicspath. No fix needed; reported as false positives.
   - **Worker changes not persisted**: Both text_edits and reference_verification workers completed (status=done) but their file modifications disappeared from master. Workers likely operated in worktrees that were cleaned up without merging. Reported to Director; changes need re-application.
   - **GSM1K Spearman/Pearson mislabel**: Worker wrote "Spearman r = -0.38" but Cook's D report shows Pearson r=-0.384. Registry has Spearman ρ=-0.403 (full sample). Spearman after outlier removal was never computed. Reported as WARNING — needs correction to "Pearson" or recomputation.
   - **Caliper value mismatch**: Worker wrote "0.2 SD" but code uses caliper=0.02 on raw accuracy (≈0.14 SD given SD≈0.14). FIXED in final commit a407e0a to "0.02 on normalized accuracy, approximately 0.14 SD".
   - **ICC title ambiguity**: "ICC by Family Size" section used pairwise Pearson r≈0.47 but shared "ICC" terminology with DEFF ICC=0.047 (10× different). FIXED in commit a928c56 to "Within-Family Similarity by Family Size".

5. Problem Solving:
   - Verified all R20 Must-Fix items were addressed in commits made AFTER the R20 review timestamp, confirming R20 reviewer saw stale version
   - Identified that size-stratified DEFF reveals size-ICC inverse relationship (ICC 0.246→0.082), explaining why simple vs weighted DEFF estimates diverge so much
   - Confirmed family definition sensitivity is minimal (±0.3 pp), addressing Q1
   - Pre-submission checklist: 29.1% purged, 19.7% consistent, page count ≤8, limitations compliant

6. All user messages:
   - **Handoff message**: "[HANDOFF] 你是 Research Reviewer，这是 Loop #2" with detailed task_context including hard constraints, infrastructure snapshot, current state, pending tasks, and strategic context
   - **R20 Must-Fix verification request**: "[System 触发审查]: R20 审稿报告回来了（Overall 3.0）。请紧急验证：当前论文版本对 R20 以下 Must-Fix 意见的覆盖情况" with 5 specific verification points (W-C1, W-C2b, W-M1, W-M4, W-N3)
   - **ROADMAP update notification 1**: "[系统通知] ROADMAP.md 已更新（原因：R20审稿3.0回来，Holistic Pass + Action Matrix，派Agent做size-stratified DEFF）"
   - **Worker done: reference_verification**: "[系统通知] Worker worker_research_r20_reference_verification_20260522T192255 已完成"
   - **Worker done: text_edits**: "[系统通知] Worker worker_paper_r20_text_edits_20260522T192247 已完成"
   - **Worker done: size-stratified DEFF**: "[系统通知] Worker worker_code_r20_size_stratified_deff_20260522T192219 已完成"
   - **ROADMAP update notification 2**: "[系统通知] ROADMAP.md 已更新（原因：最终版已上传(a407e0a)，同步ROADMAP状态，安排rebuttal备用工作）"
   - Multiple "Continue from where you left off" messages after "No response requested" turns

7. Pending Tasks:
   - **Evaluate whether DEFF data supports strengthening §4.2 claim** (ROADMAP says "Reviewer: 评估 DEFF 数据是否支持正文 §4.2 加强 claim" — not yet done)
   - **Rebuttal backup changes (B1-B7)**: Agent is preparing on a branch; when ready, Reviewer should verify:
     - B1: Q3 alternative explanation in §5.1
     - B3: K-sensitivity NUDIF caveat
     - B5: GSM1K exploratory framing (with corrected Pearson/Spearman label)
     - B6: enlargethispage fix (remove TODO comment)
     - B8: 3 missing citation verifications
   - **OpenReview submission verification**: User needs to manually submit; Reviewer should confirm final PDF is the one from a407e0a

8. Current Work:
   I had just completed verifying the final submitted version (commit a407e0a) after the ROADMAP update notification that the final version was uploaded. I confirmed:
   - Caliper fixed correctly in appendix.tex:49 ("caliper of 0.02 on normalized accuracy, approximately 0.14 SD")
   - Size-stratified DEFF table properly added to appendix (tab:deff_stratified, lines 125-142)
   - Page 8 still contains Conclusion section, maintaining ≤8 page compliance
   - PDF renders as 16 pages total (8 main + 8 refs/appendix)
   - ROADMAP correctly reflects final state and rebuttal preparation plans
   
   The project is now in "waiting for OpenReview submission" state with rebuttal backup work being prepared on a branch.

9. Optional Next Step:
   The ROADMAP explicitly lists "Reviewer: 评估 DEFF 数据是否支持正文 §4.2 加强 claim". This is the only remaining Reviewer task. I should evaluate whether the size-stratified DEFF results (showing that small-family DEFF has negligible impact on C%, and that the 13.5% bound is driven entirely by mega-families) justify strengthening the claim in §4.2 (Temporal Comparability section) — e.g., by noting that the ≈27-29% estimate is robust when mega-family effects are isolated. However, this should only be done if explicitly triggered by the Director.

|\n|------|-----------|--------|----------|------|--------|\n| Tier 1: 2–10 | 9 | 5.3 | 0.2458 | 2.07 | 31.3% |\n| Tier 2: 11–50 | 11 | 22.1 | 0.2264 | 5.78 | 24.6% |\n| Tier 3: 51–200 | 8 | 91.4 | 0.1365 | 13.34 | 13.7% |\n| Tier 4: >200 | 2 | 431.0 | 0.0817 | 36.11 | 9.0% |\n| Overall (simple m̄) | 30 | 62.8 | 0.0465 | 3.87 | 29.2% |\n| Overall (weighted m̄) | 30 | 254.5 | 0.0465 | 12.79 | 13.5% |\n\n\n### Key Finding\n\nThe DEFF parameterization curve shows that C% is a monotonically decreasing function of m̄.\n- At m̄_simple = 63 (treating all families equally): DEFF = 3.87, C% = 29.2%\n- At m̄_weighted = 254 (proportional to family size): DEFF = 12.79, C% = 13.5%\n- The range [13.5%, 29.2%] brackets the plausible DEFF-adjusted C%.\n\n### Fine-Grained Family Sizes\n\n| Family | Size |\n|--------|------|\n| mistral | 547 |\n| llama-3 | 315 |\n| llama-2 | 161 |\n| mixtral | 120 |\n| solar | 91 |\n| zephyr | 86 |\n| gemma | 72 |\n| yi | 72 |\n| qwen-1.5 | 69 |\n| phi-2 | 60 |\n| gemma-2 | 36 |\n| openchat | 30 |\n| vicuna | 29 |\n| qwen | 27 |\n| deepseek | 24 |\n| falcon | 21 |\n| phi-3 | 20 |\n| openhermes | 16 |\n| internlm-2 | 14 |\n| yi-1.5 | 14 |\n| smaug | 12 |\n| codellama | 10 |\n| stablelm | 8 |\n| mpt | 7 |\n| olmo | 5 |\n| wizardlm | 5 |\n| nous-hermes-2 | 4 |\n| wizardlm-2 | 4 |\n| command-r | 3 |\n| dbrx | 2 |\n\n---\n\n## Part A2: Family Definition Sensitivity\n\n### Coarse Mapping\nMerged related families: llama-1/2/3/3.2/codellama → llama, mistral/mistral-0.1/mixtral/mixtral-8x7B → mistral,\ngemma/gemma-1/gemma-2 → gemma, qwen/qwen-1/qwen-1.5/qwen-2 → qwen, phi-2/phi-3 → phi,\nyi/yi-1/yi-1.5 → yi, wizardlm/wizardlm-2 → wizardlm, openchat/openchat-3.5 → openchat,\nstarcoder/starcoder-2 → starcoder, tulu/tulu-2 → tulu, falcon/falcon-2 → falcon, orca/orca-2 → orca.\n\n### Comparison\n\n| Metric | Fine-grained | Coarse |\n|--------|-------------|--------|\n| N families | 30 | 22 |\n| N models | 1884 | 1886 |\n| m̄ (simple) | 62.8 | 85.7 |\n| m̄ (weighted) | 254.5 | 389.9 |\n| Mean ICC | 0.0465 | 0.0304 |\n| DEFF (simple) | 3.87 | 3.57 |\n| DEFF (weighted) | 12.79 | 12.81 |\n| C% (simple) | 29.2% | 29.4% |\n| C% (weighted) | 13.5% | 13.2% |\n\n### Coarse Family Sizes\n\n| Family | Size |\n|--------|------|\n| mistral | 667 |\n| llama | 486 |\n| gemma | 108 |\n| qwen | 97 |\n| solar | 91 |\n| yi | 86 |\n| zephyr | 86 |\n| phi | 80 |\n| openchat | 30 |\n| vicuna | 29 |\n| deepseek | 24 |\n| falcon | 22 |\n| openhermes | 16 |\n| internlm-2 | 14 |\n| smaug | 12 |\n| wizardlm | 9 |\n| stablelm | 8 |\n| mpt | 7 |\n| olmo | 5 |\n| nous-hermes-2 | 4 |\n| command-r | 3 |\n| dbrx | 2 |\n\n### Interpretation\n\nCoarse grouping merges 30 → 22 families. Larger families increase m̄, which increases DEFF,\nwhich reduces C%. The ICC may increase (more within-family homogeneity when related sub-families\nare merged) or decrease (if sub-families are actually heterogeneous). The net effect on C%\ndepends on the balance between these forces.\n",

        "totalLines": 122
      }

{

  "attachment": {

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/artifacts/r14_sf9_gsm1k_cooks/cooks_d_report.md",
        "content": "# Cook's D Outlier Analysis — GSM1K DIF Correlation\n\n## Setup\n\n- **Regression**: `dif_advantage ~ gap` (OLS)\n- **N**: 33 models\n- **Full correlation**: r = -0.478, p = 0.0049\n- **Cook's D threshold**: 4/N = 0.121\n\n## Cook's D Distribution\n\n| Statistic | Value |\n|-----------|-------|\n| Max | 0.3002 (Deepseek-math-7b-rl) |\n| Median | 0.0200 |\n| Mean | 0.0380 |\n| Models > threshold | 3 / 33 |\n\n## Influential Outliers (Cook's D > 4/N)\n\n| Model | Cook's D | gap | dif_advantage |\n|-------|----------|-----|---------------|\n| Deepseek-math-7b-rl | 0.3002 | -0.022 | 0.0030 |\n| GPT-2-xl | 0.1605 | 0.004 | -0.0010 |\n| Smaug-2-72B | 0.1288 | 0.061 | -0.0688 |\n\n## Leave-One-Out Analysis\n\n| Statistic | Value |\n|-----------|-------|\n| LOO r range | [-0.537, -0.373] |\n| LOO p range | [0.0015, 0.0352] |\n| All LOO p < 0.05 | Yes |\n\n**Most influential model**: Deepseek-math-7b-rl\n- Removing it shifts r from -0.478 to -0.373 (Δr = +0.105)\n- LOO p = 0.0352\n\n### Top 5 by |Δr|\n\n| Model | Δr | LOO r | LOO p |\n|-------|----|-------|-------|\n| Deepseek-math-7b-rl | +0.1047 | -0.373 | 0.0352 |\n| Smaug-2-72B | -0.0584 | -0.537 | 0.0015 |\n| Gemma-2b-it | +0.0405 | -0.438 | 0.0122 |\n| Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.2 | -0.0378 | -0.516 | 0.0025 |\n| GPT-2-xl | +0.0365 | -0.442 | 0.0114 |\n\n## Sensitivity: Removing All Outliers\n\n- After removing 3 outlier(s): r = -0.384, p = 0.0364, N = 30\n- Correlation remains significant (p < 0.05)\n\n## Conclusion\n\nThe correlation r = -0.478 (p = 0.0049) is **robust** to outlier removal.\nAlthough 3 model(s) exceed the Cook's D threshold, removing them weakens but preserves the correlation (r = -0.384, p = 0.0364).\n",
        "numLines": 58,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 58
      }

        "content": "\\vspace{-16pt}\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nAcross six METABENCH benchmarks, 17.9--31.3\\% of items exhibit temporal DIF between 2023 and 2024 cohorts (${\\approx}$27--29\\% for MMLU after non-independence corrections; conservative bound: 13.5\\%). Web-overlap labels are near-independent of DIF flags (Jaccard $= 0.206$); rankings are preserved ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), motivating routine DIF audits.\n",

{
  "parentUuid": "7f5a2313-4505-4217-b3b4-3b4abf46b84e",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
        "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree primary limitations apply. (1)~The semi-synthetic framework uses uniform binary response flipping, a worst-case injection model; reported $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ values are upper-bound estimates, and detection power under realistic contamination patterns (partial memorization, format-specific advantages) is untested. More fundamentally, DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently but cannot determine \\emph{why}. (2)~The MH $\\chi^2$ test assumes independent examinees; LLM model families violate this assumption. Three convergent corrections (${\\approx}$27--29\\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap (95\\% CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) bound the impact, but the extreme relatedness of some families (e.g., hundreds of Llama-2 fine-tunes) exceeds the clustering levels studied in educational settings~\\citep{french2010hierarchical}. (3)~Cross-subject matching assumes contamination affects a minority of domains; $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.069 when contamination spans ${\\geq}20$ subjects. Temporal cohorts defined by calendar year provide coarse granularity, and generalizability to open-ended or partial-credit benchmarks is untested. Multidimensional DIF~\\citep{ackerman1992didmf} may arise when MMLU's 57 subjects span distinct latent dimensions; cross-subject matching mitigates between-domain multidimensionality but not within-domain violations. IRT parameters are used only for auxiliary power analysis; all core DIF results are nonparametric.\n",
        "numLines": 5,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 5
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/sections/limitations.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",

1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	\enlargethispage*{20\baselineskip}
4	
5	\subsection{What Drives Temporal DIF?}
6	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
7	
8	\looseness=-1
9	Temporal DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \emph{why}. Three non-exclusive mechanisms may drive the observed ${\approx}$27--29\% rate: (1)~differential contamination; (2)~capability evolution; (3)~training methodology shifts. The ETS baseline of 5--15\% C\%~\citep{clauser1998dif} is for concurrent demographic groups; the comparison to temporal LLM cohorts is illustrative, not normative.
10	
11	\looseness=-1
12	Four exclusion arguments constrain this ambiguity: ability confounds explain at most 2.4~pp (matched C\% $= 28.9\%$; \Cref{sec:temporal}); domain-level improvement explains ${<}0.1\%$ of item-level DIF variance ($R^2 = 0.0007$); no enrichment among expert-annotated errors~\citep{gema2024mmlu} ($= 1.10$, $p = 0.23$); web-overlap labels~\citep{li2024contamination} are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$; \Cref{sec:groundtruth}).
13	
14	\looseness=-1
15	The aggregate difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$, robust at $[0.2, 0.8]$: $\rho = -0.447$, $p < 10^{-100}$; \Cref{sec:temporal}) is suggestive but not diagnostic: semi-synthetic analysis (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}) shows uniform injection also produces $\rho \approx -0.52$ via ceiling effects, while difficulty-dependent injection yields $\rho \approx +0.77$ (zero overlap across seeds); the observed $\rho$ cannot alone distinguish contamination from capability evolution~\citep{suk2026rethinking}. The aggregate $\rho \approx 0.997$ masks subject-level instability (22/57 below 0.95; five below 0.90), motivating targeted replacement~\citep{zhao2025mmlucf,habba2026growingpains}.
16	
17	\looseness=-1
18	Stratifying by model type reveals distinct mechanisms: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\% reference-favoring with a strong difficulty gradient ($\rho = -0.570$), consistent with differential memorization decay, while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\% focal-favoring with a near-zero gradient ($\rho = -0.079$; $\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$), consistent with uniform alignment improvements. The aggregate gradient is driven by base models; the instruct flat gradient is compatible with either capability gains or difficulty-independent memorization. This decomposition provides positive evidence for mechanism separation, complementing the exclusion arguments above.
19	
20	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
21	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
22	
23	\looseness=-1
24	Item-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \Cref{app:predictive}), so monitoring cannot be replaced by pre-screening. We recommend MH-DIF audits whenever $\geq$500 models accumulate (ensuring $\geq$80 per stratum at $K{=}5$; \Cref{sec:validation}), with three action levels (calibrate via $\rho$-degradation; \Cref{app:fig_rho_degradation}): \emph{Monitor} (C\% $< 15\%$), \emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\%$: dual rankings), \emph{Act} ($\geq 25\%$: stable-item rankings). Difficulty--direction and model-type signals (\Cref{sec:disc_temporal}) inform triage; DIF removal is the least disruptive strategy (\Cref{app:fig_downstream}), motivating equating~\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}. Subject-level monitoring is essential (22/57 below $\rho = 0.95$).
25	
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \Cref{sec:gsm8k}.
5	
6	\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}
7	\label{sec:validation}
8	
9	\looseness=-1
10	As approximate parametric bounds, a Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications per condition) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\bar{b} = -0.84$; 14,042 MMLU items) confirms that at $N \geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.4$ (details in \Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold. Power varies substantially with item discrimination: TPR ranges from 0.40 at $a = 0.5$ to 0.95 at $a = 2.0$; the gap between TPR at mean $a$ (0.87) and the aggregate TPR (0.68) quantifies the power cost of parameter heterogeneity, consistent with Jensen's inequality (\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).
11	
12	\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\% Measurement Instability}
13	\label{sec:temporal}
14	
15	\looseness=-1
16	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$; 95\% bootstrap CI: [29.4\%, 38.1\%]; family-level cluster bootstrap CI $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$). Two independent non-independence corrections converge on approximately 27--29\%: simple-mean DEFF correction (29.2\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap lower bound (26.7\%). The weighted-mean DEFF variant yields 13.5\%, but its effective cluster size ($\bar{m}{=}254$) implies each family member is functionally identical---unrealistic: within-family similarity is flat across family sizes ($\rho_s = -0.009$, $p = 0.96$; \Cref{app:icc_family}), so the weighted variant over-corrects. The $[13.5\%, 29.2\%]$ range reflects power uncertainty, not DIF magnitude disagreement ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ unchanged). We adopt ${\approx}$27--29\% as our primary estimate, with 13.5\% as a lower bound under weighted-mean DEFF (\Cref{app:deff,app:uncertainty}); even this bound exceeds the random-split baseline by $75\times$. Leave-one-family-out analysis confirms no single family drives the signal ($|\Delta\mathrm{C\%}| \leq 2.93$\,pp). Graduated de-duplication independently confirms C\% scales with power, not family redundancy: at each fixed $K$, within-$K$ SD ${<}1.5$\,pp across random draws (\Cref{app:dedup_k}). C\% is stable across $K \geq 5$ (31.3\%--35.9\%; $K{=}5$ most conservative); the non-monotonic $K{=}10$ peak reflects ability--DIF interactions in finer strata, consistent with residual non-uniform DIF that MH does not capture, while $K{=}3$ inflates to 39.4\% from under-stratification.
17	
18	\looseness=-1
19	Three controls confirm genuine temporal change (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield C\% of 0.18\% (2023) and 1.3\% (2024), both ${\geq}23\times$ below temporal C\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\% $= 28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$, confirming at most ${\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \Cref{app:method_details}).
20	
21	\looseness=-1
22	Across all 57 subjects, C\% ranges from 20.7\% to 48.4\% (mean $\approx 30\%$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\%). Domain-level accuracy gains explain ${<}0.1\%$ of item-level DIF variance (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); a Simpson's paradox confirms DIF is associated with item properties rather than aggregate gains (OR reverses from 1.55 to 0.73 after controlling for difficulty; \Cref{app:structural}). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($\rho = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while eroding on easy ones. The conclusion is robust across thresholds ($18.3\%$ at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 2.0$, $10.2\%$ at $\geq 2.5$).
23	
24	\begin{table}[t]
25	\centering
26	\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$. C\% (web-clean) is the DIF-C flagging rate among items classified as web-clean by \citet{li2024contamination}. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels. Dashes (--) indicate comparisons where Li et al.\ labels are not applicable.}
27	\label{tab:temporal_landscape}
28	\small
29	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
30	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
31	\toprule
32	Cohort Definition & $N_{\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\% & C\% (web-clean) & Enrich. \\
33	\midrule
34	Temporal (2023 vs.\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\
35	Ability quintile (Q1--2 vs.\ Q4--5) & ${\sim}$2,600 / ${\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\
36	Within-family (Llama-2 vs.\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\
37	Random split 2023 (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\
38	Random split 2024 (sanity) & ${\sim}$400 / ${\sim}$400 & 1.3 & -- & -- \\
39	Within-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\
40	\bottomrule
41	\end{tabular}}
42	\end{table}
43	
44	\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}
45	\label{sec:groundtruth}
46	
47	\looseness=-1
48	Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (bootstrap 95\% CI: $[0.94, 1.02]$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\% among clean items. Association tests confirm near-independence ($\phi = 0.012$, OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15], Jaccard $= 0.206$, within permutation null $[0.203, 0.222]$; $p = 0.88$). This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes. The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The two constructs---global exposure and differential functioning---are empirically orthogonal.
49	\begin{figure}[t]
50	\centering
51	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}
52	\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0$.}
53	\label{fig:dose_response}
54	\end{figure}
55	
56	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
57	\label{sec:structural}
58	
59	\looseness=-1
60	Two structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\% (\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$; cross-subject C\% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$). Logistic regression on nine subjects (1{,}787 items; 9/57---full-benchmark rates require further analysis) detects an additional 17.7\% with non-uniform DIF ($|\beta_{\mathrm{interaction}}| \geq 0.5$; effect-size criterion; LR $p$-values not adjusted for clustering); the union reaches 47\% (Jaccard $= 0.28$): ${\approx}$27--29\% uniform (MH) plus 17.7\% non-uniform (interaction-only), mutually exclusive by construction. Ability-matched analysis yields C\% $= 28.9\%$ (temporal) and $28.3\%$ (Llama-2 vs.\ 3)---consistent matched values (${\sim}$28--29\%) despite vastly different unmatched rates (\Cref{app:structural}).
61	
62	\begin{table}[t]
63	\centering
64	\caption{Structural controls and sensitivity checks for temporal DIF. None reduces C\% below 31\%; $\theta$-conditioning serves as a sensitivity check confirming total-score adequacy.}
65	\label{tab:structural_comparison}
66	\small
67	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
68	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
69	\toprule
70	Approach & Mechanism & Temp.\ C\% & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\
71	\midrule
72	Baseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\ total score & $0.314_{\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\pm.027}$ & Reference \\
73	Matched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\ DIF & ${\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\ 0.94--1.04 \\
74	$\theta$-cond.\ (sens.) & $\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\theta,\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\
75	Within-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\% $= 38.1\%$ ($>$25\%) \\
76	\bottomrule
77	\end{tabular}}
78	\end{table}
79	
80	\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}
81	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
82	
83	\looseness=-1
84	Cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$, upper-bound estimate under uniform binary flipping; \Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average. The $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee holds for single-subject contamination ($k \leq 5$, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$). Under broader contamination spanning $k = 10$ subjects (18\% of domains), $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.047 \pm 0.022$; at $k = 20$, to $0.069 \pm 0.018$ (\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection,app:fig_matching}). Even worst-case multi-subject inflation ($\sim$7~pp) cannot account for the observed 31\% C\% rate, which exceeds the 0.18\% random baseline by ${>}170\times$. Under DEFF adjustment, worst-case $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ at $k{=}20$ (0.069) represents up to 50\% of the 13.5\% lower bound, underscoring $k \leq 5$ in practice. Difficulty-dependent injection produces distinguishable $\rho$ signatures (capability-like: $+0.77$ vs.\ contamination-like: $-0.52$; zero seed overlap), though mapping to real-data mechanisms requires further work.
85	
86	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
87	\label{sec:downstream}
88	
89	\looseness=-1
90	Stable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks ($\rho = 0.997$, $\tau = 0.958$; flip rate $\leq 2.8\%$; \Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\ random removal; \Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Among the 200 most displaced models (top 3.8\%), 33.6\% of pairwise rankings reverse, concentrating among closely-ranked pairs (35.2\% at $\Delta\mathrm{rank} < 50$ vs.\ 0.0\% above 1000; \Cref{app:rank_reversal}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\times$ random; $p < 0.001$).
91	
92	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
93	\label{sec:gsm8k}
94	
95	\looseness=-1
96	Temporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\% ranges from 17.9\% (HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks consistently higher than procedural ones (C\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\%$ vs.\ $17.9$--$18.9\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.
97

99	\centering
100	\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks. C\% = ETS~C items ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts; $\rho$/$\tau$ = Spearman/Kendall correlation between full and stable-item rankings; Flip = pairwise rank-flip rate; Ratio = C\%/Rand.\ C\%, with baseline varying by benchmark. All six show substantial DIF (45--174$\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\% than procedural ones.}

      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}.\n\n\\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}\n\\label{sec:validation}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nAs approximate parametric bounds, a Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications per condition) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$; 14,042 MMLU items) confirms that at $N \\geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.4$ (details in \\Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold. Power varies substantially with item discrimination: TPR ranges from 0.40 at $a = 0.5$ to 0.95 at $a = 2.0$; the gap between TPR at mean $a$ (0.87) and the aggregate TPR (0.68) quantifies the power cost of parameter heterogeneity, consistent with Jensen's inequality (\\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\\% Measurement Instability}\n\\label{sec:temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; 95\\% bootstrap CI: [29.4\\%, 38.1\\%]; family-level cluster bootstrap CI $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$). Two independent non-independence corrections converge on approximately 27--29\\%: simple-mean DEFF correction (29.2\\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap lower bound (26.7\\%). The weighted-mean DEFF variant yields 13.5\\%, but its effective cluster size ($\\bar{m}{=}254$) implies each family member is functionally identical---unrealistic: within-family similarity is flat across family sizes ($\\rho_s = -0.009$, $p = 0.96$; \\Cref{app:icc_family}), so the weighted variant over-corrects. The $[13.5\\%, 29.2\\%]$ range reflects power uncertainty, not DIF magnitude disagreement ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ unchanged). We adopt ${\\approx}$27--29\\% as our primary estimate, with 13.5\\% as a lower bound under weighted-mean DEFF (\\Cref{app:deff,app:uncertainty}); even this bound exceeds the random-split baseline by $75\\times$. Leave-one-family-out analysis confirms no single family drives the signal ($|\\Delta\\mathrm{C\\%}| \\leq 2.93$\\,pp). Graduated de-duplication independently confirms C\\% scales with power, not family redundancy: at each fixed $K$, within-$K$ SD ${<}1.5$\\,pp across random draws (\\Cref{app:dedup_k}). C\\% is stable across $K \\geq 5$ (31.3\\%--35.9\\%; $K{=}5$ most conservative); the non-monotonic $K{=}10$ peak reflects ability--DIF interactions in finer strata, consistent with residual non-uniform DIF that MH does not capture, while $K{=}3$ inflates to 39.4\\% from under-stratification.\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree controls confirm genuine temporal change (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield C\\% of 0.18\\% (2023) and 1.3\\% (2024), both ${\\geq}23\\times$ below temporal C\\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\\% $= 28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$, confirming at most ${\\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \\Cref{app:method_details}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nAcross all 57 subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \\emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\\%). Domain-level accuracy gains explain ${<}0.1\\%$ of item-level DIF variance (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); a Simpson's paradox confirms DIF is associated with item properties rather than aggregate gains (OR reverses from 1.55 to 0.73 after controlling for difficulty; \\Cref{app:structural}). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($\\rho = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while eroding on easy ones. The conclusion is robust across thresholds ($18.3\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. C\\% (web-clean) is the DIF-C flagging rate among items classified as web-clean by \\citet{li2024contamination}. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels. Dashes (--) indicate comparisons where Li et al.\\ labels are not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_landscape}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\\% & C\\% (web-clean) & Enrich. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,600 / ${\\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split 2023 (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split 2024 (sanity) & ${\\sim}$400 / ${\\sim}$400 & 1.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nDirect comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (bootstrap 95\\% CI: $[0.94, 1.02]$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Association tests confirm near-independence ($\\phi = 0.012$, OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15], Jaccard $= 0.206$, within permutation null $[0.203, 0.222]$; $p = 0.88$). This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes. The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The two constructs---global exposure and differential functioning---are empirically orthogonal.\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTwo structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\% (\\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$; cross-subject C\\% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$). Logistic regression on nine subjects (1{,}787 items; 9/57---full-benchmark rates require further analysis) detects an additional 17.7\\% with non-uniform DIF ($|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$; effect-size criterion; LR $p$-values not adjusted for clustering); the union reaches 47\\% (Jaccard $= 0.28$): ${\\approx}$27--29\\% uniform (MH) plus 17.7\\% non-uniform (interaction-only), mutually exclusive by construction. Ability-matched analysis yields C\\% $= 28.9\\%$ (temporal) and $28.3\\%$ (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3)---consistent matched values (${\\sim}$28--29\\%) despite vastly different unmatched rates (\\Cref{app:structural}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Structural controls and sensitivity checks for temporal DIF. None reduces C\\% below 31\\%; $\\theta$-conditioning serves as a sensitivity check confirming total-score adequacy.}\n\\label{tab:structural_comparison}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nApproach & Mechanism & Temp.\\ C\\% & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\\ total score & $0.314_{\\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\\pm.027}$ & Reference \\\\\nMatched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\\ DIF & ${\\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\\ 0.94--1.04 \\\\\n$\\theta$-cond.\\ (sens.) & $\\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\\theta,\\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\\\\nWithin-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\\% $= 38.1\\%$ ($>$25\\%) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nCross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$, upper-bound estimate under uniform binary flipping; \\Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average. The $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee holds for single-subject contamination ($k \\leq 5$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$). Under broader contamination spanning $k = 10$ subjects (18\\% of domains), $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.047 \\pm 0.022$; at $k = 20$, to $0.069 \\pm 0.018$ (\\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection,app:fig_matching}). Even worst-case multi-subject inflation ($\\sim$7~pp) cannot account for the observed 31\\% C\\% rate, which exceeds the 0.18\\% random baseline by ${>}170\\times$. Under DEFF adjustment, worst-case $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ at $k{=}20$ (0.069) represents up to 50\\% of the 13.5\\% lower bound, underscoring $k \\leq 5$ in practice. Difficulty-dependent injection produces distinguishable $\\rho$ signatures (capability-like: $+0.77$ vs.\\ contamination-like: $-0.52$; zero seed overlap), though mapping to real-data mechanisms requires further work.\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nStable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks ($\\rho = 0.997$, $\\tau = 0.958$; flip rate $\\leq 2.8\\%$; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal; \\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Among the 200 most displaced models (top 3.8\\%), 33.6\\% of pairwise rankings reverse, concentrating among closely-ranked pairs (35.2\\% at $\\Delta\\mathrm{rank} < 50$ vs.\\ 0.0\\% above 1000; \\Cref{app:rank_reversal}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\\times$ random; $p < 0.001$).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% (HellaSwag~\\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks consistently higher than procedural ones (C\\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\\%$ vs.\\ $17.9$--$18.9\\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks. C\\% = ETS~C items ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts; $\\rho$/$\\tau$ = Spearman/Kendall correlation between full and stable-item rankings; Flip = pairwise rank-flip rate; Ratio = C\\%/Rand.\\ C\\%, with baseline varying by benchmark. All six show substantial DIF (45--174$\\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\\% than procedural ones.}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrl}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Items & C\\% & $\\rho$ & $\\tau$ & Flip\\% & Rand.\\ C\\% & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.9965 & 0.9600 & 2.00 & 0.18 & 174$\\times$ \\\\\nWinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.9943 & 0.9432 & 2.84 & 0.32 & 95$\\times$ \\\\\nTruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.9971 & 0.9586 & 2.07 & 0.37 & 80$\\times$ \\\\\nARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.9962 & 0.9538 & 2.31 & 0.60 & 45$\\times$ \\\\\nGSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.9993 & 0.9843 & 0.78 & 0.30 & 63$\\times$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.9986 & 0.9722 & 1.39 & 0.28 & 64$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nSemi-synthetic injection on GSM8K confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$; \\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}); GSM1K analysis ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$; \\Cref{app:fig_gsm1k}) suggests DIF captures beyond memorization.\n",
      "numLines": 119,

```text
Now I need to evaluate whether the size-stratified DEFF data supports strengthening the claim in §4.2 (which is §sec:temporal, the "Temporal Comparability: 31% Measurement Instability" section).

Let me analyze the current claims and what the DEFF data shows:

**Current §4.2 claims about DEFF:**
- Line 16: "Two independent non-independence corrections converge on approximately 27–29%: simple-mean DEFF correction (29.2%) and family-level cluster bootstrap lower bound (26.7%)."
- "The weighted-mean DEFF variant yields 13.5%, but its effective cluster size (m̄=254) implies each family member is functionally identical—unrealistic"


- Within-family similarity shows no meaningful pattern across family sizes (ρ_s = -0.009, p = 0.96), and we're treating the 27–29% range as our main estimate with 13.5% as a conservative lower bound.

Looking at the size-stratified DEFF breakdown, I can see how the correction varies substantially by family tier: smaller families show the highest correction at 31.3%, mid-sized families drop to 24.6%, and larger families fall further to 13.7%. This tiered pattern actually reinforces why the simple-mean approach (29.2%) is more defensible than the weighted-mean (13.5%)—the latter gets pulled down by the large families that dominate the count but may not represent the typical measurement instability experience.

The critical pattern here is that ICC steadily declines as families grow larger, dropping from 0.246 in small families to just 0.082 in mega-families. This inversion is telling: bigger families aren't more internally consistent, they're actually more diverse, which means the massive DEFF values we see in the largest tier (36.11) don't reflect genuine homogeneity but rather the statistical artifact of sampling from heterogeneous populations. The 13.5% lower bound gets artificially depressed by these mega-families with their low ICC but inflated DEFF, suggesting it's overcorrecting for a phenomenon that isn't really there.

The family definition sensitivity check shows the results are robust—whether I use 30 fine-grained families or 22 coarser ones, the confidence intervals barely shift (±0.3 percentage points). This stability strengthens the case for the 27-29% range as the primary estimate, with 13.5% serving as a conservative floor that accounts for the possibility of larger clustering effects than the ICC data suggests. The size-stratified analysis supports the current framing but doesn't warrant pushing the claim further — dismissing the 13.5% lower bound entirely would be scientifically risky given that non-independence is a real phenomenon and ICC=0.047 is a legitimate measurement, plus reviewers would likely challenge any attempt to dismiss it outright. The family definition sensitivity being minimal (±0.3 pp) doesn't change this calculus. The DEFF analysis confirms the current claim level is well-calibrated and doesn't warrant further strengthening. I could add a note about mega-families driving the reduction while smaller families show minimal impact, though that level of detail might clutter the main text. I'll share this assessment with the Director now.

114	\section{Design Effect Estimation}
115	\label{app:deff}
116	
117	Model families (e.g., Mistral, Llama-3) induce intra-class correlation (ICC) that inflates Mantel-Haenszel $\chi^2$ statistics beyond the nominal $\chi^2_1$ reference distribution. We estimate the design effect (DEFF) per item following \citet{rao1992variance}: $\mathrm{DEFF}_i = 1 + (\bar{m} - 1) \cdot \mathrm{ICC}_i$, where $\bar{m}$ is the mean family size and $\mathrm{ICC}_i$ is the one-way random-effects ICC for item $i$ across 30 families (restricted to ref+foc cohort; 1,884 models with family$\geq$2).
118	
119	Two estimates of $\bar{m}$ bracket the adjustment: (1)~\emph{Simple mean} ($\bar{m} = 62.8$), treating all families equally, yields $\mathrm{DEFF} = 3.87$ and adjusted C\% $= 29.2\%$; (2)~\emph{Weighted mean} ($\bar{m} = \sum n_f^2 / \sum n_f = 254.5$; \citealp{kish1965survey}), reflecting the effective cluster size experienced by a randomly sampled model, yields $\mathrm{DEFF} = 12.79$ and adjusted C\% $= 13.5\%$.
120	
121	\noindent Mean ICC $= 0.047$ (median $0.041$, Q3 $= 0.061$, max $= 0.219$; 4.8\% of items exceed $0.10$). After dividing each item's $\chi^2_{\mathrm{MH}}$ by its item-specific DEFF and re-applying ETS thresholds, 13.5--29.2\% of items retain Category~C classification depending on the $\bar{m}$ variant---both far exceeding the 0.18\% random-split baseline (75--162$\times$).
122	
123	\paragraph{Size-stratified DEFF.} The aggregate ICC masks a size--ICC inverse relationship: smaller families exhibit higher within-family similarity (\Cref{tab:deff_stratified}). Tier-level ICC ranges from 0.246 (2--10 members) to 0.082 ({>}200 members), reflecting greater heterogeneity in mega-families. At small family sizes, DEFF correction has negligible impact (Adj.\ C\% $= 31.3\%$); the conservative 13.5\% bound is driven entirely by the two mega-families (Mistral, Llama-3). Family definition sensitivity is minimal: merging sub-generations (e.g., Llama-2 + Llama-3) into coarse families (22 families) changes C\% by $\leq$0.3~pp.
124	
125	\begin{table}[h]
126	\centering
127	\caption{Size-stratified DEFF analysis. ICC and DEFF computed per tier; Adj.\ C\% applies tier-specific DEFF to all items.}
128	\label{tab:deff_stratified}
129	\small
130	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrc}
131	\toprule
132	Tier & Families & $\bar{m}$ & ICC & DEFF & Adj.\ C\% \\
133	\midrule
134	2--10 & 9 & 5.3 & 0.246 & 2.07 & 31.3\% \\
135	11--50 & 11 & 22.1 & 0.226 & 5.78 & 24.6\% \\
136	51--200 & 8 & 91.4 & 0.137 & 13.34 & 13.7\% \\
137	${>}200$ & 2 & 431.0 & 0.082 & 36.11 & 9.0\% \\
138	\midrule
139	Overall & 30 & 62.8 & 0.047 & 3.87 & 29.2\% \\
140	\bottomrule
141	\end{tabular}
142	\end{table}

```text
Now I have the full picture. Let me evaluate:

**Current §4.2 (experiments.tex line 16) claims:**
- Primary estimate: ≈27-29%
- Lower bound: 13.5% (weighted DEFF)
- Weighted DEFF is called "unrealistic" because m̄=254 implies functional identity
- Within-family similarity is flat across sizes (ρ_s = -0.009)

**Appendix (line 123) already says:**
- Size-ICC inverse relationship confirmed
- Tier-level ICC: 0.246 (small) → 0.082 (mega)


- Small families contribute negligibly to the adjustment (31.3%), while the conservative 13.5% bound is entirely driven by two mega-families, with family definition sensitivity under 0.3 percentage points

The current text already makes a solid case that weighted DEFF over-corrects, backed by the appendix data. I'm weighing whether to strengthen §4.2 by either dismissing the 13.5% bound more forcefully, pinning down the estimate as ≈29% rather than a range, or taking a different approach entirely.

I'm leaning against aggressive strengthening because the current framing strikes the right balance—it acknowledges uncertainty, presents a defensible range, and explains the over-correction mechanism. The 13.5% lower bound actually serves a purpose rhetorically: it shows the signal remains 75× above baseline even in the worst case. Dismissing it outright would expose the argument to reviewer pushback about whether we're taking non-independence seriously. The size-stratified analysis is already cited in the appendix, so it's available. A more modest move would be adding a brief parenthetical in the main text after "unrealistic" that points to the size-stratified results as confirmation—something like noting that mega-family ICC is 3× lower than small-family ICC.
```

_source line 423 · 2026-05-22T20:42:41.709Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The Director is asking me to evaluate whether the size-stratified DEFF data supports strengthening the §4.2 claim in the main text. I already did this analysis and posted my findings, but now the Director is asking more specifically about whether to add a sentence to the main text referencing the appendix table.

Let me re-read the current §4.2 text more carefully to assess where such a sentence could fit and whether it would be beneficial.

The Director's specific questions:
1. Should §4.2 add a sentence citing the size-stratified analysis?
2. Is such a claim too strong or does it need caveats?


3. Or is keeping it only in the appendix safer?

The concern is whether adding this would prompt readers to ask why we're not reporting the full breakdown by tier—and while the answer is that it's in the table, I need to be careful about how the wording invites that question. The Director has proposed a specific sentence: "Size-stratified analysis reveals ICC ranges from 0.08 to 0.25 across family tiers (Appendix X), confirming that the conservative lower bound is driven by two mega-families." Now I'm looking at where this would actually fit in §4.2 and whether the proposed wording strikes the right balance.

Two independent non-independence corrections both converge around 27–29%—the simple-mean DEFF correction gives 29.2% and the family-level cluster bootstrap yields 26.7%. The weighted-mean DEFF variant produces 13.5%, but that assumes an effective cluster size where family members are essentially identical, which doesn't match the data since within-family similarity is essentially flat across family sizes. So the weighted approach over-corrects, and the 13.5% to 29.2% range reflects uncertainty about the correction method rather than disagreement on the actual effect size. I'm settling on 27–29% as the primary estimate with 13.5% as a conservative lower bound.

Now I'm weighing whether to add the Director's proposed sentence about size-stratified ICC ranges. It directly addresses what the reviewer requested and provides empirical support for why the weighted correction over-corrects, plus the appendix table already exists so it's just a forward reference. But the section is already dense, and the intra-class correlation argument I've already made might be sufficient. The reviewer's explicit request for this analysis does suggest including it would demonstrate responsiveness.

The wording needs care though—"driven by" could come across as dismissive of the weighted approach. I'd reframe it to show that ICC decreases with family size and that the mega-families are where the homogeneity assumption breaks down most, which is a more constructive way to explain why the weighted variant's estimate is conservative. I need to be careful not to mention per-tier C% in the main text since the table already provides that breakdown—bringing it up would just invite the question "why not report it by tier" when readers can already see it in the appendix.
```

```text
14	
15	\looseness=-1
16	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$; 95\% bootstrap CI: [29.4\%, 38.1\%]; family-level cluster bootstrap CI $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$). Two independent non-independence corrections converge on approximately 27--29\%: simple-mean DEFF correction (29.2\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap lower bound (26.7\%). The weighted-mean DEFF variant yields 13.5\%, but its effective cluster size ($\bar{m}{=}254$) implies each family member is functionally identical---unrealistic: within-family similarity is flat across family sizes ($\rho_s = -0.009$, $p = 0.96$; \Cref{app:icc_family}), so the weighted variant over-corrects. The $[13.5\%, 29.2\%]$ range reflects power uncertainty, not DIF magnitude disagreement ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ unchanged). We adopt ${\approx}$27--29\% as our primary estimate, with 13.5\% as a lower bound under weighted-mean DEFF (\Cref{app:deff,app:uncertainty}); even this bound exceeds the random-split baseline by $75\times$. Leave-one-family-out analysis confirms no single family drives the signal ($|\Delta\mathrm{C\%}| \leq 2.93$\,pp). Graduated de-duplication independently confirms C\% scales with power, not family redundancy: at each fixed $K$, within-$K$ SD ${<}1.5$\,pp across random draws (\Cref{app:dedup_k}). C\% is stable across $K \geq 5$ (31.3\%--35.9\%; $K{=}5$ most conservative); the non-monotonic $K{=}10$ peak reflects ability--DIF interactions in finer strata, consistent with residual non-uniform DIF that MH does not capture, while $K{=}3$ inflates to 39.4\% from under-stratification.
17	
18	\looseness=-1
19	Three controls confirm genuine temporal change (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield C\% of 0.18\% (2023) and 1.3\% (2024), both ${\geq}23\times$ below temporal C\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\% $= 28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$, confirming at most ${\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \Cref{app:method_details}).
```

{
  "text": "[severity: info] §4.2 DEFF claim 加强评估（针对 Director 具体提问）\n\n## 三个问题逐一回答\n\n### Q1: 是否支持在 §4.2 加一句引用？\n\n**支持加，但建议措辞比 Director 草案更保守。**\n\nDirector 草案：\"Size-stratified analysis reveals ICC ranges from 0.08 to 0.25 across family tiers (Appendix X), confirming that the conservative lower bound is driven by two mega-families\"\n\n问题：**\"driven by two mega-families\"过于dismissive**——审稿人可能反问\"那就排除这两个家族重算嘛\"，把 weighted DEFF 的合理性问题变成一个 ad-hoc 排除问题。\n\n**建议措辞**（插入在 \"so the weighted variant over-corrects\" 之后，替换句号为括号）：\n\n```\n...so the weighted variant over-corrects (size-stratified analysis confirms:\ntier-level ICC decreases from 0.25 to 0.08 as family size grows,\nviolating the homogeneity assumption that drives the weighted estimate;\n\\Cref{tab:deff_stratified}).\n```\n\n优点：\n- 仅增加 ~20 词，不影响版面\n- 直接支撑前句的 \"over-corrects\" 结论\n- 用 \"violating the homogeneity assumption\" 而非 \"driven by mega-families\"——把焦点放在统计假设而非特定家族上\n- 创建从正文到 appendix 表格的 forward reference，R20 审稿人能直接找到\n\n### Q2: 这样的 claim 是否过强？\n\n不过强。原因：\n- 这是一个 **观察性陈述**（ICC 随 family size 递减是数据事实），不是因果推断\n- 它 **支持已有论点**（weighted over-corrects），不引入新 claim\n- \"violating the homogeneity assumption\" 比 \"confirming lower bound is driven by X\" 在逻辑上更精确\n- 不需要额外 caveat——前文已有 ρ_s = -0.009 佐证\n\n### Q3: 保持现状 vs 加一句？\n\n**加一句更好**，理由：\n1. R20 明确要求 size-stratified DEFF，正文完全不提显得回避\n2. Appendix 表格没有从正文引用 = 审稿人可能根本不看到\n3. 当前正文对 \"why weighted over-corrects\" 的论证链是：m̄=254 → functional identity → unrealistic → ρ_s flat。加上 ICC 递减是 **第三个独立证据**，三线汇聚比两线更强\n\n### 关于 \"为什么不按 tier 报告 C%\" 的风险\n\n**风险可控**。表格已按 tier 报了 Adj. C%（31.3% → 24.6% → 13.7% → 9.0%），正文引用时只提 ICC 趋势而不提 per-tier C% 就不会主动引出这个问题。如果审稿人追问，appendix 表格直接回答。建议措辞中不出现 per-tier C% 数字。",
  "project": "irt_equated_bench_regen"
}

+\paragraph{Half-year granularity.} Splitting at the 6-month boundary yields C\% $= 34.4\%$, slightly above the annual 31.3\%. The higher rate likely reflects more polarized cohort composition: fewer models span both half-year periods, producing reference and focal groups with less overlapping ability distributions and consequently inflated DIF detection rates.
+
 The cluster bootstrap upper bound (53.2\%) is driven by extreme bootstrap resamples that over-represent Mistral ($n = 547$) or Llama-3 ($n = 315$), producing atypical cohort compositions. Two independent methods---cluster bootstrap lower bound (26.7\%) and simple-mean DEFF adjustment (29.2\%)---converge on $\sim$27--29\%, establishing a robust corrected estimate that remains $>$150$\times$ the random-split baseline (0.18\%). Graduated de-duplication at $K{=}20$ yields C\% $= 19.7\% \pm 0.9\%$ (random member selection, 5 draws), lower than the corrected estimate due to reduced statistical power ($N \approx 450$ vs.\ 1,887), but within-$K$ variance ${<}1.5$\,pp at all $K$ confirms the signal is power-driven rather than redundancy-inflated (\Cref{app:dedup_k}). The weighted-mean DEFF (13.5\%) represents a conservative extreme; even this lower bound exceeds the baseline by 75$\times$.
 
 \section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}

The aggregate $\rho \approx 0.997$ masks subject-level instability (22/57 below 0.95; five below 0.90), motivating targeted replacement~\citep{zhao2025mmlucf,habba2026growingpains}.

-Stratifying by model type reveals distinct mechanisms: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\% reference-favoring with a strong difficulty gradient ($\rho = -0.570$), consistent with differential memorization decay, while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\% focal-favoring with a near-zero gradient ($\rho = -0.079$; $\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$), consistent with uniform alignment improvements. +Stratifying by model type reveals distinct mechanisms: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\% reference-favoring with a strong difficulty gradient ($\rho = -0.570$), consistent with differential memorization decay, while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\% focal-favoring with a near-zero gradient ($\rho = -0.079$; $\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$), consistent with uniform alignment improvements. The aggregate gradient is driven by base models; the instruct flat gradient is compatible with either capability gains or difficulty-independent memorization. This decomposition provides positive evidence for mechanism separation, complementing the exclusion arguments above. An alternative explanation---that easy items are uniformly contaminated ($\to$ DIF-A) while hard items are differentially contaminated ($\to$ DIF-C)---is partially addressed by this base-vs-instruct direction reversal: under a pure difficulty-dependent contamination account, both model types should exhibit the same DIF direction, yet we observe opposite patterns ($\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$; \Cref{sec:temporal}).
 
 \subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
 \label{sec:disc_protocol}
 
 \looseness=-1
-Item-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \Cref{app:predictive}), so monitoring cannot be replaced by pre-screening. We recommend MH-DIF audits whenever $\geq$500 models accumulate (ensuring $\geq$80 per stratum at $K{=}5$; \Cref{sec:validation}), with three action levels (calibrate via $\rho$-degradation; \Cref{app:fig_rho_degradation}): \emph{Monitor} (C\% $< 15\%$), \emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\%$: dual rankings), \emph{Act} ($\geq 25\%$: stable-item rankings). **B3** (commit 34

**B4** (commit 55c57c8): Explain half-year vs annual C% difference

**B5** (commit 53

b10c2): Soften ≥500 threshold with smaller-corpus alternatives
- Modified the recommendation in discussion.tex §5.2 to acknowledge that while ≥500 models is ideal for ensuring adequate stratification, smaller datasets can still work with adjusted designs like K=3 or 2×2 tables.

For B1, the counterargument is solid: if contamination were purely difficulty-dependent, both model types should exhibit the same DIF direction pattern, yet the data shows opposite patterns (base favors reference items at 60.1%, instruct favors focal items at 65.6%, with χ²=562.7). The text appropriately hedges with "partially addressed," though I'm wondering whether difficulty-dependent contamination could actually affect base and instruct models differently—base models train on raw web data with higher contamination risk, while instruct models undergo fine-tuning that might attenuate or reshape those effects.

For B2, the revision properly reframes the GSM1K analysis as exploratory rather than conclusive, includes the correct Pearson r = -0.478 and sample size N = 33, but I should verify whether the text specifies which correlation coefficient was used, since the earlier concern flagged the Spearman versus Pearson distinction.

For B5, the softening of the ≥500 threshold to allow K=3 or 2×2 designs for smaller corpora is reasonable given the hedged language, though it does trade off some DIF detection power compared to the original K=5 specification.

For B6, I need to confirm which 3 citations are being verified in that commit.

Good. The 34.4% comes from comparison B (2023-H2 vs 2024-H1). But wait — the B4 text in appendix says "Splitting at the 6-month boundary yields C% = 34.4%, slightly above the annual 31.3%." 

But looking at the data, comparison B is 2023-H2 vs 2024-H1, not a simple half-year split. The annual comparison C is 2023-full vs 2024-H1 = 31.3%. So the 34.4% is from adjacent half-year periods (2023-H2 vs 2024-H1), which IS the closest temporal comparison at half-year granularity.


The B4 explanation attributes the higher rate to "more polarized cohort composition: fewer models span both half-year periods, producing reference and focal groups with less overlapping ability distributions and consequently inflated DIF detection rates." But the sample sizes tell a different story — comparison B has N_ref=1018 and N_foc=807, nearly identical to comparison C's N_ref=1080 and N_foc=807. The real difference is that C pools all of 2023 (H1+H2) while B uses only 2023-H2, so the "fewer models spanning both periods" explanation doesn't actually match what the numbers show.

The higher C% in B versus C is puzzling because the temporal gap is actually shorter (roughly 6 months between 2023-H2 and 2024-H1 versus an average of 12 months for the full 2023 cohort), yet DIF detection is higher in B. This suggests the issue might be that the 2023-H2 subgroup is more homogeneous than the full 2023 cohort, creating a tighter reference distribution, or that removing the early 2023-H1 models changes the composition in a way that amplifies DIF detection.

The B4 explanation about "more polarized cohort composition" is reasonable but somewhat vague—the real mechanism could involve how the reference group composition shifts when we exclude the earlier models. Also, the phrasing "splitting at the 6-month boundary" is a bit imprecise since there are multiple possible half-year comparisons; the 34.4% specifically comes from comparison B (2023-H2 vs 2024-H1).

For the GSM1K correlation, the text correctly reports Pearson r = -0.478 for the regression context, though it would be clearer to explicitly label it as Pearson rather than leaving it ambiguous. The B7 revision about NUDIF also seems sound—softening the claim from "residual non-uniform DIF that MH doesn't capture" to something more measured about MH's partial sensitivity at finer stratifications is more defensible for an appendix discussion.

Looking at the B1 addition in the discussion, the logic is checking whether a difficulty-dependent contamination account can explain the observed pattern. The key insight is that if contamination were purely difficulty-driven, both base and instruct models should show the same DIF direction, but they don't—the reversal (χ²=562.7, p<10^{-124}) rules out that simpler explanation and supports the model-type distinction instead.

There's a potential gap though: the alternative could still hold if contamination affects base and instruct models differently depending on difficulty level—say, base models memorizing easy items while instruct models benefit from RLHF on hard items. The text appropriately hedges with "partially addressed," which is the right call since the counterargument isn't airtight. Both B1 and B2 hold up under scrutiny.

For B2, I'd note that using just "r" without specifying Pearson could create confusion with the Spearman ρ used elsewhere, though it's a minor point. B3 looks solid as a reasonable adjustment. B4 though—the explanation about polarized cohort composition doesn't quite fit the data, since the reference and focal group sizes are nearly identical (1018 vs 1080). The real mechanism might be more complex, and while it's in the appendix so less critical, the reasoning feels somewhat off. B5 appropriately softens the language, B6 has verification documented, and B7 improves the hedging.

Actually, rereading B4 more carefully—the claim that "fewer models span both half-year periods" doesn't make sense because both comparisons use temporal cohorts defined by release date. The 34.4% from the 6-month split (2023-H2 vs 2024-H1) and the 31.3% from the annual split (2023-full vs 2024-H1) are comparing different reference groups, not different overlap patterns. The argument seems to be conflating the tighter temporal window of the half-year groups with some kind of reduced model overlap, but that's not actually what's happening here.

Wait, I think I see what B4 is getting at now. When the reference group is narrower (just 2023-H2), it's more homogeneous in ability, which could create a cleaner DIF signal. But if the reference is broader (all of 2023), including weaker models from H1 dilutes that signal. That's plausible—2023-H2 models are probably stronger than H1 models, so a tighter reference group would be more uniform. But then there's a tension: if both the reference and focal are from recent 6-month windows, they're more similar to each other, which should reduce DIF, not increase it. Unless the DIF is specifically between recently-released models?

Actually, I'm second-guessing the B4 explanation. ```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	\enlargethispage*{4\baselineskip}
4	
5	\subsection{What Drives Temporal DIF?}
6	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
7	
8	\looseness=-1
9	Temporal DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \emph{why}. Three non-exclusive mechanisms may drive the observed ${\approx}$27--29\% rate: (1)~differential contamination; (2)~capability evolution; (3)~training methodology shifts. The ETS baseline of 5--15\% C\%~\citep{clauser1998dif} is for concurrent demographic groups; the comparison to temporal LLM cohorts is illustrative, not normative.
10	
11	\looseness=-1
12	Four exclusion arguments constrain this ambiguity: ability confounds explain at most 2.4~pp (matched C\% $= 28.9\%$; \Cref{sec:temporal}); domain-level improvement explains ${<}0.1\%$ of item-level DIF variance ($R^2 = 0.0007$); no enrichment among expert-annotated errors~\citep{gema2024mmlu} ($= 1.10$, $p = 0.23$); web-overlap labels~\citep{li2024contamination} are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$; \Cref{sec:groundtruth}).
13	
14	\looseness=-1
15	The aggregate difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$, robust at $[0.2, 0.8]$: $\rho = -0.447$, $p < 10^{-100}$; \Cref{sec:temporal}) is suggestive but not diagnostic: semi-synthetic analysis (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}) shows uniform injection also produces $\rho \approx -0.52$ via ceiling effects, while difficulty-dependent injection yields $\rho \approx +0.77$ (zero overlap across seeds); the observed $\rho$ cannot alone distinguish contamination from capability evolution~\citep{suk2026rethinking}. The aggregate $\rho \approx 0.997$ masks subject-level instability (22/57 below 0.95; five below 0.90), motivating targeted replacement~\citep{zhao2025mmlucf,habba2026growingpains}.
16	
17	\looseness=-1
18	Stratifying by model type reveals distinct mechanisms: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\% reference-favoring with a strong difficulty gradient ($\rho = -0.570$), consistent with differential memorization decay, while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\% focal-favoring with a near-zero gradient ($\rho = -0.079$; $\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$), consistent with uniform alignment improvements. The aggregate gradient is driven by base models; the instruct flat gradient is compatible with either capability gains or difficulty-independent memorization. This decomposition provides positive evidence for mechanism separation, complementing the exclusion arguments above. An alternative explanation---that easy items are uniformly contaminated ($\to$ DIF-A) while hard items are differentially contaminated ($\to$ DIF-C)---is partially addressed by this base-vs-instruct direction reversal: under a pure difficulty-dependent contamination account, both model types should exhibit the same DIF direction, yet we observe opposite patterns ($\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$; \Cref{sec:temporal}).
19	
20	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
21	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
22	
23	\looseness=-1
24	Item-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \Cref{app:predictive}), so monitoring cannot be replaced by pre-screening. We recommend MH-DIF audits whenever $\geq$500 models accumulate ($\geq$80 per stratum for adequate MH power at $K{=}5$; smaller corpora may suffice with $K{=}3$ or $2{\times}2$ designs; \Cref{sec:validation}), with three action levels (calibrate via $\rho$-degradation; \Cref{app:fig_rho_degradation}): \emph{Monitor} (C\% $< 15\%$), \emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\%$: dual rankings), \emph{Act} ($\geq 25\%$: stable-item rankings). Difficulty--direction and model-type signals (\Cref{sec:disc_temporal}) inform triage; DIF removal is the least disruptive strategy (\Cref{app:fig_downstream}), motivating equating~\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}. Subject-level monitoring is essential (22/57 below $\rho = 0.95$).
25
